## Cab (id: 4376)

CAB
===

A *cab* was a dry measure used in Bible times. The Jewish historian Josephus said it was about the size of a quart (about one liter). Some experts think it may have been larger (see [2 Kings 6:25](https://ref.ly/2Kgs6:25)).

*See* Kab.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Kings 6:25
* **Associated Articles:** Kab (ID: `6509@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Cabbon (id: 4374)

CABBON
======

Cabbon was a town in the foothills of Judah ([Joshua 15:40](https://ref.ly/Josh15:40)) east of Lachish. It has been identified with Hebra and is sometimes considered the same place as Macbenah ([1 Chronicles 2:49](https://ref.ly/1Chr2:49)).

* **Associated Passages:** Joshua 15:40; 1 Chronicles 2:49

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cabbon (ID: `place:Cabbon`)

## Cabul (id: 4375)

CABUL
=====

1. A town in Asher, near Mount Carmel on the border between Israel and Tyre ([Joshua 19:27](https://ref.ly/Josh19:27)).
2. The territory that Solomon gave Hiram, king of Tyre. In return, Hiram gave 120 talents (about four metric tons or 9,000 pounds) of gold to complete the temple. Hiram was not impressed with this northern Galilean province ([1 Kings 9:13–14](https://ref.ly/1Kgs9:13-1Kgs9:14)). He later returned it to Solomon ([2 Chronicles 8:2](https://ref.ly/2Chr8:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Joshua 19:27; 2 Chronicles 8:2; 1 Kings 9:13-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cabul (ID: `place:Cabul.2`); Cabul (ID: `place:Cabul`)

## Caesarea (id: 4378)

CAESAREA
========

Caesarea was a city built by Herod the Great between 22 and 10 BC and named in honor of Augustus Caesar. It covered about 3,240 hectares (8,000 acres) and stood on the Mediterranean coast, about 40 kilometers (25 miles) south of modern Haifa, in the plain of Sharon.

Caesarea Maritima was another name for this city. It became the administrative center of the country. This happened during the period when Rome occupied Israel. Three Roman governors of Palestine lived there: 

* Felix ([Acts 24](https://ref.ly/Acts24:1-Acts24:27)),
* Festus ([25:1, 4–6, 13](https://ref.ly/Acts25:1,Acts25:4-Acts25:6,Acts25:13)), and
* Pontius Pilate.

Pilate visited Jerusalem on special occasions (see [John 19](https://ref.ly/John19:1-John19:42)). Archaeologists found an inscription bearing Pilate’s name in the theater at Caesarea.

Caesarea was the major seaport of Judea in New Testament times. The southern Palestinian coastline lacked a good harbor. So, Herod created one by building two huge breakwaters. These could protect ships from storms on the Mediterranean Sea.

Caesarea in the New Testament
-----------------------------

A Roman officer named Cornelius converted to Christianity in Caesarea ([Acts 10:1, 24](https://ref.ly/Acts10:1,Acts10:24)). Later, the apostle Peter visited Philip. He was a deacon, evangelist, and prominent Christian leader who lived there ([21:8](https://ref.ly/Acts21:8)). Paul spent more than two years in prison in Caesarea ([24:27–25:1](https://ref.ly/Acts24:27-Acts25:1)). Then he sailed from there on his journey to Rome (chapter [27](https://ref.ly/Acts27:1-Acts27:44)). 

In AD 70, the Roman general Titus returned to Caesarea after conquering Jerusalem. In AD 73, Roman general Flavius Silva went there after defeating the fortress cities of Masada and Herodium. Both of these cities were in eastern Judea.

Archaeological Discoveries at Caesarea
--------------------------------------

Excavations carried out since 1971 have greatly increased knowledge of Caesarea.

Herod built a high\-level aqueduct to bring fresh water from Mount Carmel to the city. The water came from springs northeast of Caesarea and traveled through an underground channel to Mount Carmel. A smaller aqueduct carried slightly salty water from a spring north of the city for irrigation.

Archaeologists have also found large sewers beneath the city. The Jewish historian Josephus mentioned these sewers, which were flushed by the movement of the sea.

A hippodrome, or racetrack, stood on the east side of the city and could hold about 30,000 people. It was probably built in the second century AD. It was destroyed during the Muslim conquest in AD 640, along with a large government records building near the coast. Excavations of the records building uncovered several inscriptions in its mosaic floors. Two of them quoted the Greek text of [Romans 13:3](https://ref.ly/Rom13:3). A large amphitheater also lies northwest of the hippodrome. It remains underground and has been seen only through infrared photography.

Excavations in 1976 uncovered the first evidence of Strato’s Tower. According to Josephus, this was the Hellenistic settlement near which Herod built Caesarea.

Archaeologists also uncovered a small synagogue north of a large Crusader fort near the harbor built by Herod. The harbor area contained many stone storerooms. Seven have been entered, but as many as 73 may still remain unexcavated.

One storeroom was later reused by Roman soldiers as a Mithraeum, a center for the worship of the Persian god Mithras. It is the only Mithraeum found in Palestine.

Caesarea was not rebuilt after Muslim forces destroyed the city in the thirteenth century.

*See also* Herod, Herodian Family.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 10:1; Acts 10:24; Acts 21:8; Acts 25:1; Acts 25:13; Romans 13:3; Acts 24:1-27; Acts 25:4-6; John 19:1-42; Acts 24:27-25:1; Acts 27:1-44
* **Associated Articles:** Herod, Herodian Family (ID: `5821@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea.2`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`)

## Caesarea Philippi (id: 4377)

CAESAREA PHILIPPI
=================

A city at the northern end of Palestine. It is located on the southern slopes of Mount Hermon, near the ancient city of Dan. Caesarea Philippi is in a beautiful area near one of the three sources of the Jordan River, the Wadi Banias.

Early History
-------------

In the second century BC, the place was called Panion because the Greek god Pan was worshiped in a cave there. A Greek historian named Polybius describes Panion. He identifies Panion as the place where Syrian king Antiochus III defeated the Ptolemies of Egypt. This was an important battle in about 200 BC. 

The Jewish historian Josephus (*Antiquities* 15\.10\.3\) wrote that “Panium” was governed by Zenodorus. This place of worship was “a very fine cave in a mountain. Under the mountain, there is a great hole in the earth. The hole opens quickly and is very deep. It is full of still water. A vast mountain covers the cave. The springs of the Jordan river rise from under the caves.”

Under Roman Rule
----------------

After the death of Zenodorus, Augustus Caesar gave the city to Herod the Great. Josephus says Herod “made this place very beautiful. It was already a very beautiful place.” It had a "beautiful temple made of white stone.” 

When Herod died in 4 BC, his son Philip was given the territory surrounding Panion. The area was known as Paneas. In *War* 2\.9\.1, Josephus reported that “Philip built the city Caesarea, at the fountains of Jordan, and in the region of Paneas.” Philip made it his capital. He named it Caesarea Philippi after the Roman emperor Tiberius Caesar and himself. This helped separate it from the larger Caesarea Maritima on the Mediterranean coast. 

In *War* 3\.9\.7, Josephus wrote that emperors Vespasian and Titus both “marched from Caesarea by the sea to Caesarea Philippi.”

Peter's Confession
------------------

Caesarea Philippi is where the apostle Peter confessed (declared is belief) that Jesus is “the Christ, the Son of the living God” ([Matthew 16:13–16](https://ref.ly/Matt16:13-Matt16:16); [Mark 8:27–29](https://ref.ly/Mark8:27-Mark8:29)).

Later History
-------------

About AD 50, Agrippa II made Caesarea Philippi larger. He renamed the city Neronias to honor the emperor Nero. Today, the city is called Banias because Arabic speakers found it difficult to pronounce the older name Paneas.

* **Associated Passages:** Matthew 16:13-16; Mark 8:27-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Caesarea Philippi (ID: `place:CaesareaPhilippi`)

## The Caesars (id: 4380)

THE CAESARS
===========

The Caesars were the rulers of the Roman Empire. The name Caesar was first the family name of Julius Caesar, who lived 100–44 BC.

Later Roman leaders used "Caesar" as a title, even if they were not from Julius Caesar’s family. This is where the words *Kaiser* (in German), *Keizer* (in Dutch), and *Czar* (in Russian) come from.

The Bible mentions Caesar Augustus ([Luke 2:1](https://ref.ly/Luke2:1)) and Tiberius Caesar ([Luke 3:1](https://ref.ly/Luke3:1)). In the book of Acts, the title "Caesar" refers to Nero ([Acts 25:11–12, 21](https://ref.ly/Acts25:11-Acts25:12,Acts25:21); [26:32](https://ref.ly/Acts26:32); [27:24](https://ref.ly/Acts27:24); [28:19](https://ref.ly/Acts28:19)). During the time of the New Testament, 12 Caesars ruled. Six of them were part of the original Caesar family.

Emperors from Caesar’s Lineage
------------------------------

The following rulers belonged to the family line or political legacy associated with Julius Caesar:

* Julius Caesar (100–44 BC). He was not officially an emperor, but his name and leadership began the line of rulers known as the Caesars.
* Tiberius (42 BC–AD 37\). He ruled from AD 14 to 37\.
* Claudius (10 BC–AD 54\). He ruled from AD 41 to 54\.
* Nero (AD 37–68\). He ruled from AD 54 to 68\.

Some Later Emperors
-------------------

The following Roman emperors ruled after the emperors from Caesar’s line:

* Galba (3 BC–AD 69\). He ruled AD 68–69\.
* Vespasian (AD 9–79\). He ruled AD 69–79\.
* Titus (AD 39–81\). He ruled AD 79–81\.
* Domitian (AD 51–96\). He ruled AD 81–96\.
* Trajan (AD 53–117\). He ruled AD 98–117\.
* Diocletian (AD 245–313\). He ruled AD 284–305\.
* Constantine the Great (AD 272 or 273–337\). He ruled AD 306–337\.

*See also* City of Rome.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 1:1; Luke 2:1; Luke 3:1; John 19:12; Acts 5:37; Acts 11:28; Acts 12:22; Acts 18:2; Acts 25:11; Acts 25:21; Acts 26:32; Acts 27:24; Acts 28:19; 1 Peter 4:12; Acts 12:1-5; Acts 12:1-25; John 18:33-38; Philippians 1:7-14; Acts 25:11-12; Acts 25:1-26:32
* **Associated Articles:** Claudius (ID: `4584@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Domitian (ID: `4882@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Nero (ID: `7368@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Tiberius (ID: `8833@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Vespasian (ID: `9009@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Rome (City) (ID: `8062@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Julius Caesar (ID: `787495@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Galba (ID: `787496@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Titus Caesar (ID: `787497@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Trajan (ID: `787498@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Diocletian (ID: `787499@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Constantine the Great (ID: `787500@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Caesar’s Household (id: 4379)

CAESAR’S HOUSEHOLD
==================

Caesar’s household was the group of people who worked for the Roman emperor. This included both slaves and free people. They lived in Rome and in other parts of the Roman Empire.

The apostle Paul greeted "those from the household of Caesar” in one of his letters ([Philippians 4:22](https://ref.ly/Phil4:22)). Caesar was the title of the emperor of Rome. The emperor’s household had hundreds of workers. Many of their jobs were respected and important in Roman society.

When Paul arrived in Rome, many people from Caesar’s household welcomed him. This is reported in a second\-century writing called the Martyrdom of Paul. Paul also spoke with Jewish leaders in Rome and taught openly about Jesus. He preached without being stopped ([Acts 28:17, 31](https://ref.ly/Acts28:17,Acts28:31)). Some men and women believed his message ([Acts 28:23–24](https://ref.ly/Acts28:23-Acts28:24)). Some of these new believers were likely from Caesar’s household. The message about Jesus even reached the whole Praetorian Guard ([Philippians 1:13](https://ref.ly/Phil1:13)), a special group of Roman soldiers who guarded the emperor. 

Some Bible scholars believe that certain people mentioned in [Romans 16](https://ref.ly/Rom16:1-Rom16:27) may have also belonged to the emperor’s household.

*See also* Caesars, The.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:17; Acts 28:31; Philippians 1:13; Philippians 4:22; Acts 28:23-24; Romans 16:1-27
* **Associated Articles:** The Caesars (ID: `4380@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Caiaphas (id: 4381)

CAIAPHAS
========

Caiaphas was the high priest during the life and ministry of Jesus. As the leader of the Jewish nation, he headed the Sanhedrin, the highest court. Next to the Roman governor, Caiaphas was the most powerful man in Judea, responsible to the Romans for the nation's behavior. He was particularly worried about the excitement and unrest surrounding Jesus, especially with the growing activities of the Zealots, who would soon start a rebellion.

The raising of Lazarus ([John 11](https://ref.ly/John11:1-John11:57)) caused strong public reaction and brought the conflict to a high point. Caiaphas feared that people who wanted a political messiah would lead to a Roman crackdown, so he argued that Jesus should die ([John 11:48](https://ref.ly/John11:48-John11:50)[–](https://ref.ly/John11:48-John11:50)[50](https://ref.ly/John11:48-John11:50)). The Gospel of John notes that, in doing so, Caiaphas unknowingly prophesied about the atoning nature of the death of Jesus ([John 11:51](https://ref.ly/John11:51-John11:52)[–](https://ref.ly/John11:51-John11:52)[52](https://ref.ly/John11:51-John11:52)).

Caiaphas played a central role in the arrest and trial of Jesus. The religious leaders made their plans in his palace ([Matthew 26:3](https://ref.ly/Matt26:3-Matt26:5)[–](https://ref.ly/Matt26:3-Matt26:5)[5](https://ref.ly/Matt26:3-Matt26:5)), and part of the trial took place there with Caiaphas presiding ([Matthew 26:57](https://ref.ly/Matt26:57-Matt26:68)[–](https://ref.ly/Matt26:57-Matt26:68)[68](https://ref.ly/Matt26:57-Matt26:68)). Before this, Jesus was first brought to Annas, Caiaphas's father\-in\-law ([John 18:13](https://ref.ly/John18:13)). Although Matthew, Mark, and Luke do not mention the visit to Annas, and Mark and Luke do not refer to Caiaphas by name, John's account shows Annas was still influential.

When Jesus admitted he was "the Christ, the Son of God," Caiaphas tore his robes and accused him of blasphemy ([Matthew 26:63](https://ref.ly/Matt26:63-Matt26:66)[–](https://ref.ly/Matt26:63-Matt26:66)[66](https://ref.ly/Matt26:63-Matt26:66)). After Pentecost, Caiaphas, along with other Jewish leaders, led the trial of Peter and John, trying to stop the apostles' preaching ([Acts 4:5](https://ref.ly/Acts4:5-Acts4:6)[–](https://ref.ly/Acts4:5-Acts4:6)[6](https://ref.ly/Acts4:5-Acts4:6)).

Annas, a former high priest, remained important in Jewish affairs, which explains why Luke mentions both Annas and Caiaphas in connection with John the Baptist's ministry ([Luke 3:2](https://ref.ly/Luke3:2)) and in Acts called Annas the high priest ([Acts 4:6](https://ref.ly/Acts4:6)). John's Gospel also shows Annas was still commonly called "high priest" ([John 18:22](https://ref.ly/John18:22)).

According to the historian Josephus, Caiaphas was appointed high priest around AD 18 and served until he was removed around AD 36\. Since the high priest served at the Romans' discretion, Caiaphas's long tenure suggests he was politically skilled. After being removed by the Roman proconsul Vitellus, nothing more is known about him.

* **Associated Passages:** Luke 3:2; John 18:13; John 18:22; Acts 4:6; Matthew 26:57-68; John 11:1-57; John 11:48-50; John 11:51-52; Matthew 26:3-5; Matthew 26:63-66; Acts 4:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Caiaphas (ID: `person:Caiaphas`)

## Cain (Person) (id: 4383)

CAIN (Person)
=============

The first son of Adam and Eve. He became a tiller of the soil while his brother, Abel, was a keeper of sheep. When people talk about terrible acts of violence, they often mention Cain's murder of his brother Abel as an example ([Jude 1:11](https://ref.ly/Jude1:11)). Each of the two brothers had brought a sacrifice to the Lord ([Genesis 4:3–4](https://ref.ly/Gen4:3-Gen4:4)). According to [Hebrews 11:4](https://ref.ly/Heb11:4), Abel had acted in faith by bringing a more acceptable sacrifice than that of Cain. Cain became very angry because God did not accept his offering. Because God accepted Abel's offering but not his own, Cain killed his brother ([Genesis 4:5–8](https://ref.ly/Gen4:5-Gen4:8)). 

When explaining why Cain acted with violence, the Bible tells us that he belonged to the evil one ([1 John 3:12](https://ref.ly/1John3:12)). The Lord confronted Cain with his guilt, judged him, and pronounced a curse upon him. God made Cain settle in the land of Nod, east of Eden ([Genesis 4:9–16](https://ref.ly/Gen4:9-Gen4:16)). Cain complained to God that his punishment was greater than he could bear. He was afraid that someone would find him and kill him. So, the Lord put a mark on Cain to protect him. The Lord also promised that if anyone killed Cain, that person would receive punishment seven times worse.

In the land of Nod, Cain built a city and named it after his son Enoch ([Genesis 4:17](https://ref.ly/Gen4:17)). Through Enoch, Cain had many descendants. In the early generations, these descendants developed different skills. Some lived in tents and took care of animals. Others became musicians, and some learned how to make things from metal (verses [18–22](https://ref.ly/Gen4:18-Gen4:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 4:17; Hebrews 11:4; 1 John 3:12; Jude 1:11; Genesis 4:3-4; Genesis 4:5-8; Genesis 4:9-16; Genesis 4:18-22
* **Associated Articles:** Abel (Person) (ID: `3281@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Adam (Person) (ID: `3363@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Eden (ID: `4939@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Enoch (Person) (ID: `5088@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Eve (ID: `5194@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Curse, Cursed (ID: `4713@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Musician (ID: `7247@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Nod (ID: `7419@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Sacrifice (ID: `8090@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cain (ID: `person:Cain`)

## Calah (id: 4385)

CALAH
=====

Calah was one of the ancient capital cities of Assyria. Genesis describes it as a city built by Nimrod ([Genesis 10:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen10:11-Gen10:12)). 

Calah is the ancient name of modern Nimrud. It lies on the east bank of the Tigris River, about 38\.6 kilometers (24 miles) south of Nineveh. Henry Layard excavated the site from 1845 to 1849\. The British School of Archaeology in Iraq carried out further excavations from 1949 to 1964\. People lived at the site from prehistoric times through the Hellenistic period.

Excavations at Calah uncovered a large ziggurat, a stepped temple tower, and temples dedicated to the gods Ninurta and Nabu.

Archaeologists also found a large fortress built by Shalmaneser I in the thirteenth century BC and a palace built by Ashurnasirpal II, who ruled from 883 to 859 BC. Parts of palaces built by Shalmaneser III and Esarhaddon were also uncovered.

One of the most important discoveries was the Black Obelisk of Shalmaneser III, now in the British Museum. It is important for biblical studies because it records that King Jehu of Israel paid tribute to Assyria.

Tiglath\-pileser III, who ruled from 745 to 727 BC, and Sargon II, who ruled from 721 to 705 BC, launched military campaigns against Israel and Judah from Calah.

Sargon captured Samaria. Tiglath\-pileser became involved in Judah’s affairs when King Ahaz sought his help against Israel and Syria ([Isaiah 7:1–17](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa7:17)). The Babylonians and Medes destroyed Calah in 612 BC.

*See also* Nimrod.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 10:11-12; Isaiah 7:1-17
* **Associated Articles:** Nimrod (ID: `7407@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Calah (ID: `place:Calah`)

## Calamus (id: 4386)

CALAMUS
=======

A sweet\-smelling cane plant ([Exodus 30:23](https://ref.ly/Exod30:23); [Ezekiel 27:19](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:19)). Calamus refers to either a plant or its aromatic root. It was one of the plants that grew in Solomon's garden ([Song of Solomon 4:14](https://ref.ly/Song4:14)).

Two plants have been suggested as the source of biblical calamus: 

* the sweet flag (*Acorus calamus*), and
* the beardgrass (*Andropogon aromaticus*).

The sweet flag has a strong pleasant smell and grows in Europe and Asia, but it is not found in Israel and the surrounding areas. Beardgrass, which comes from India, releases a strong fragrance when crushed and is thought to be the calamus mentioned in the Bible. Beardgrass produces an oil called ginger\-grass oil.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 30:23; Song of Songs 4:14; Ezekiel 27:19
* **Associated Articles:** Plants (ID: `7761@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Calamus (ID: `flora:Calamus`)

## Caleb (id: 4390)

CALEB
=====

1. A son of Jephunneh the Kenizzite ([Numbers 32:12](https://ref.ly/Num32:12); [Joshua 14:6](https://ref.ly/Josh14:6)). He was the older brother of Kenaz ([Judges 1:13](https://ref.ly/Judg1:13)). Caleb was one of the 12 spies sent to explore the land of Canaan. While most of the spies were afraid to attack, Caleb and Joshua recommended moving forward immediately. The Israelites rejected their advice because they feared the strongly defended cities. Because of this lack of faith, God delayed Israel's entrance into Canaan, the promised land, for many years ([Numbers 14:21–23, 34–35](https://ref.ly/Num14:21-Num14:23,Num14:34-Num14:35)).

    When Israel finally entered Canaan under Joshua's leadership, Caleb was 85 years old ([Joshua 14:6–7, 10](https://ref.ly/Josh14:6-Josh14:7,Josh14:10)). Joshua assigned Hebron as the territory of Caleb. Caleb conquered the Anakim who lived there (verses [13–14](https://ref.ly/Josh14:13-Josh14:14)). Caleb offered his daughter Acsah in marriage to anyone who conquered the nearby town of Kiriath\-sepher (also called Debir). His nephew Othniel (who was Acsah's cousin) conquered the city and married Acsah ([15:16–17](https://ref.ly/Josh15:16-Josh15:17)).

    Hebron later became a city of refuge for Levites ([Joshua 21:13](https://ref.ly/Josh21:13); [1 Chronicles 6:55–57](https://ref.ly/1Chr6:55-1Chr6:57)). In part of Caleb's territory, David spent time hiding from King Saul. There David met his future wife Abigail, who was then married to Nabal, a descendant of Caleb ([1 Samuel 25:3](https://ref.ly/1Sam25:3)). This area was also where David's wives were captured by Amalekite raiders who had attacked southern Judah and "the Negev of Caleb" ([1 Samuel 30:14](https://ref.ly/1Sam30:14)).

2. A son of Hezron and brother of Jerahmeel ([1 Chronicles 2:18, 42](https://ref.ly/1Chr2:18,1Chr2:42)). This Caleb is also called Chelubai (verse [9](https://ref.ly/1Chr2:9)). Many scholars think this might be the same person as the Caleb mentioned above. They believe this because:

    1. Both men have a daughter named Achsah (verse [49](https://ref.ly/1Chr2:49)).
        2. It would be unusual for an otherwise unknown Caleb to have such an important place in the family records.
        According to these scholars, Caleb is a son of Hezron and the grandson of Judah. This was to establish his position and inheritance in the tribe of Judah. But Caleb was a foreigner. He was a son of Jephunneh, a Kenizzite. He had joined himself and his clan to the tribe of Judah. Some scholars support this view. They argue that the name Caleb is Horite, not Israelite.

3. The King James Version calls Caleb a son of Hur ([1 Chronicles 2:50](https://ref.ly/1Chr2:50)). But it is likely the King James Version combines what should be two separate phrases. The Berean Standard Bible correctly shows this as two separate statements: “These were the descendants of Caleb. The sons of Hur…”

* **Associated Passages:** Numbers 32:12; Joshua 14:6; Joshua 14:10; Joshua 21:13; Judges 1:13; 1 Samuel 25:3; 1 Samuel 30:14; 1 Chronicles 2:9; 1 Chronicles 2:18; 1 Chronicles 2:42; 1 Chronicles 2:49; 1 Chronicles 2:50; Numbers 14:21-23; Numbers 14:34-35; Joshua 14:6-7; Joshua 14:13-14; Joshua 15:16-17; 1 Chronicles 6:55-57

## Calendars (Ancient and Modern) (id: 4391)

CALENDARS (ANCIENT AND MODERN)
==============================

A calendar is a system that shows when each year begins, how long it lasts, and how it is divided into days, weeks, and months.

People rarely think about how the modern calendar works. However, without a calendar, it would be very difficult to create a clear timeline of events. A timeline shows the order in which events happened. It would also be impossible to predict the changing seasons accurately.

Before the calendar reached its modern form, it went through several stages of development.

Preview
-------

•Days and Their Divisions

•Astronomy and the Calendar

•Jewish Calendar

•Jewish Festivals

•Conclusion

Days and Their Divisions
------------------------

The earliest way to measure time was probably to count days. Later, people divided each day into smaller parts. These parts eventually became the 24 equal periods called hours.

The Sumerians seem to have developed the system of measuring time in days, hours, and minutes. They also used the word “day” in a narrower sense to mean a 12\-hour period.

The stairway built by King Ahaz was used to tell time by the movement of a shadow ([2 Kings 20:9](https://ref.ly/2Kgs20:9); [Isaiah 38:8](https://ref.ly/Isa38:8)). However, people probably did not measure hours precisely until much later.

The ancient Egyptians began the day at dawn. They divided the day into two periods of 12 hours. In the second century AD, the Egyptian astronomer Ptolemy and his followers developed a system that began each day at noon. At noon, the sun is at its highest point in the sky.

The Roman day began at sunrise and had two parts. The second part of the day began at sunset.

Astronomy and the Calendar
--------------------------

Ancient peoples created calendars by observing the repeated movements of the sun and moon. A solar year is the time the earth takes to travel once around the sun.

Ancient peoples depended on the changing seasons. Their lives were shaped by changes in temperature and by the different lengths of day and night during the year.

The seasons occur because the earth travels around the sun while also turning on its axis. The earth’s axis is tilted at an angle.

In the Northern Hemisphere, the longest day of the year is called the summer solstice. It occurs around 21 June. The winter solstice occurs on 21 or 22 December. On that day, the sun appears lowest in the sky at noon, and the daylight hours are shortest.

In the Southern Hemisphere, the seasons are reversed because of the earth’s tilt.

The spring and autumn equinoxes fall between the solstices. The spring equinox occurs around 21 March, and the autumn equinox occurs around 23 September. At these times, the sun is directly above the equator, and day and night are about equal in length. The word “equinox” comes from Latin words meaning “equal night.” Ancient peoples could measure a solar year as the time between two matching solstices or two matching equinoxes.

A solar calendar measures days by observing the time between two returns of the sun to the same position in the sky. This may be from one sunrise to the next, from one sunset to the next, or from one midday to the next. A day is the time the earth takes to turn once on its axis. It is now divided into 24 hours.

However, the earth’s daily rotation does not match its yearly journey around the sun exactly. A solar year is not a whole number of days. It lasts 365 days plus part of another day. This difference creates problems when making a calendar.

The greatest calendar problems appear when both the sun and moon are used to measure the year. Ancient peoples found it difficult to make the solar year match the lunar cycle. This was especially difficult when months were based on the changing phases of the moon. The problem occurs because the moon travels around the earth while the earth travels around the sun and turns on its own axis. These different movements do not fit together exactly, so they create many calendar difficulties.

A lunar calendar measures time by lunations. A lunation is the length of time between one new moon and the next. Each lunar month begins when the thin crescent of the new moon first becomes visible at sunset. A lunar month lasts an average of a little more than 29 and a half days.

The moon travels around the earth in about 27 and one\-third days. However, during that time, the earth also moves around the sun. Because of this movement, the moon needs about two more days to return to the same position between the earth and the sun and produce another new moon.

A lunar year has 12 months and is about 11 days shorter than a solar year. To correct this difference, some calendar systems added extra days or months. This practice is called intercalation.

The ancient Chinese calendar had 12 months of either 29 or 30 days. It added an extra month every 30 years. The Muslim lunar calendar is still used throughout Islam. It also works on a 30\-year cycle. The second year of each cycle is a leap year. After that, a leap year occurs every three years. A leap year has 355 days, while an ordinary Muslim year has 354 days. The ancient Hebrew calendar had the same basic problems as other lunar calendars.

Jewish Calendar
---------------

The lives of the people of ancient Israel were closely connected to the calendar. The calendar helped organize many parts of their religious and daily life.

The Jewish calendar begins with the traditional date of creation. This date is set at 3,760 years and three months before the Christian era (the years counted from the birth of Christ). To estimate the Jewish calendar year, add 3,759 to the year in the Gregorian calendar (the calendar used in most of the world today).

However, this method does not give an exact result for every month. The Jewish civil year begins in autumn (September or October), while the Gregorian year begins in midwinter (January). 

### Months

The Jews adopted most of the 12 month names from the Babylonians after the exile. These months do not match the months of the Roman calendar exactly. Instead, they overlap with them.

The Old Testament mentions the names of more than half of these months:

* Chislev ([Nehemiah 1:1](https://ref.ly/Neh1:1); [Zechariah 7:1](https://ref.ly/Zech7:1))
* Tebeth ([Esther 2:16](https://ref.ly/Esth2:16))
* Shebat ([Zechariah 1:7](https://ref.ly/Zech1:7))
* Adar ([Esther 3:7](https://ref.ly/Esth3:7,Esth3:8); [8:12](https://ref.ly/Esth8:12))
* Nisan ([Nehemiah 2:1](https://ref.ly/Neh2:1); [Esther 3:7](https://ref.ly/Esth3:7))
* Sivan ([Esther 8:9](https://ref.ly/Esth8:9))
* Elul ([Nehemiah 6:15](https://ref.ly/Neh6:15))

The Jewish month always began with the new moon, about every 29 and a half days. For this reason, the Jewish year had 354 days. This was a lunar calendar, a calendar based on the cycles of the moon. A solar year is based on the earth’s movement around the sun. We do not know exactly how the Jews first adjusted their lunar calendar to match the solar year.

Later in Israel’s history, an extra month was added between Adar and Nisan. This month was sometimes called Veader (“second Adar”). It was added seven times during a 19\-year cycle. When this happened, Adar also received an extra half day.

The Jewish month names used today come from the period after the Jews returned from Babylonia to Palestine. Before the Babylonian exile, at least four other month names were used:

* Abib ([Exodus 13:4](https://ref.ly/Exod13:4)) later became Nisan,
* Ziv ([1 Kings 6:1, 37](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:1,1Kgs6:37)) became Iyyar,
* Ethanim ([1 Kings 8:2](https://ref.ly/1Kgs8:2)) became Tishri, and
* Bul ([1 Kings 6:38](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:38)) became Heshvan, originally called Marcheshvan.

The earlier month names were connected with farming. For example, Abib was the month when the heads of grain became ripe. Ziv was the month when flowers bloomed in the desert.

This connection with farming also appears in what seems to be the oldest known Hebrew calendar. It was found at Gezer, southeast of Tel Aviv, in 1908 and dates to the tenth century BC. The calendar was probably written by an Israelite schoolboy. It divides the year according to farming activities such as planting, harvesting, pruning, and storing crops.

The Hebrew Calendar
-------------------

*A Hebrew month usually began around the middle of a month in the modern calendar. People planted crops in November and December and harvested them in March and April.*

| ***Month*** | ***Today’s Calendar*** | ***Bible Reference*** | ***Israel’s Holidays*** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 Nisan (Abib) | March–April | [Exodus 13:4](https://ref.ly/Exod13:4); [23:15](https://ref.ly/Exod23:15); [34:18](https://ref.ly/Exod34:18); [Deuteronomy 16:1](https://ref.ly/Deut16:1) | Passover ([Leviticus 23:5](https://ref.ly/Lev23:5)) Unleavened Bread ([Leviticus 23:6](https://ref.ly/Lev23:6)) Firstfruits ([Leviticus 23:10](https://ref.ly/Lev23:10)) |
| 2 Iyyar (Ziv) | April–May | [1 Kings 6:1](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:1), [37](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:37) | Second Passover ([Numbers 9:10–11](https://ref.ly/Num9:10-Num9:11)) |
| 3 Sivan | May–June | [Esther 8:9](https://ref.ly/Esth8:9) | Pentecost (Weeks or Harvest) ([Leviticus 23:16](https://ref.ly/Lev23:16)) |
| 4 Tammuz | June–July |  |  |
| 5 Ab | July–August |  |  |
| 6 Elul | August–September | [Nehemiah 6:15](https://ref.ly/Neh6:15) |  |
| 7 Tishri (Ethanim) | September–October | [1 Kings 8:2](https://ref.ly/1Kgs8:2) | Trumpets (New Year’s Day, Rosh Hashanah) ([Leviticus 23:24](https://ref.ly/Lev23:24); [Numbers 29:1](https://ref.ly/Num19:1)) Day of Atonement ([Leviticus 23:27](https://ref.ly/Lev23:27)) Tabernacles (Shelters, Booths) ([Leviticus 23:34](https://ref.ly/Lev23:34)) |
| 8 Marcheshvan (Bul) | October–November | [1 Kings 6:38](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:38) |  |
| 9 Chislev (also spelled Kislev) | November–December | [Nehemiah 1:1](https://ref.ly/Neh1:1) | Dedication (Hanukkah) ([John 10:22](https://ref.ly/John10:22)) |
| 10 Tebeth | December–January | [Esther 2:16](https://ref.ly/Esth2:16) |  |
| 11 Shebat | January–February | [Zechariah 1:7](https://ref.ly/Zech1:7) |  |
| 12 Adar | February–March | [Esther 3:7](https://ref.ly/Esth3:7) | Purim ([Esther 9:24–32](https://ref.ly/Esth9:24-Esth9:32)) |

The months were especially important for Jewish religious life. They helped the Jewish people remember important events in their history. The beginning of each month was considered holy.

For ancient Israel, the moon became a spiritual symbol of the nation. The sun later became a symbol of the Messiah ([Malachi 4:2](https://ref.ly/Mal4:2)). The moon does not produce its own light, so this symbolism was especially meaningful. Israel was to reflect the Messiah’s light to the world.

The Jewish calendar remained unchanged during the approximately 400 years between the Old Testament and the New Testament. This was true even though Greek rulers tried to introduce a different lunar calendar, probably based on a Macedonian system.

In that calendar, each of the 12 months had 30 days, and five days were added to the final month of the year. Even with these extra days, the calendar only came close to matching the solar year.

### How Dates Were Recorded

There is no known period in which the ancient Hebrews recorded dates by giving a month and day. Instead, they dated events by referring to an important event, such as the year when the current king began to rule.

In New Testament times, the Jews continued to use the same basic method found in the Old Testament. They connected events with dates in their religious calendar or with important events in the Roman world. New Testament writers used the same method ([Luke 1:5](https://ref.ly/Luke1:5); [John 12:1](https://ref.ly/John12:1); [Acts 18:12](https://ref.ly/Acts18:12)). 

Over time, the calendar reforms introduced by Julius Caesar became widely used. People then began to move from the older dating system to a more standard system.

Jewish Festivals
----------------

In addition to keeping the Sabbath, Jews observe seven yearly festivals.

1. **Passover** takes place on the 14th day of Nisan. It remembers Israel’s deliverance from Egypt in the exodus. The first day of Nisan determines the date of Passover. Passover lasts seven days and includes the Feast of Unleavened Bread. This feast remembers how quickly the Israelites had to prepare to leave Egypt ([Exodus 12:15](https://ref.ly/Exod12:15)). The festival of the firstfruits of the barley harvest follows immediately afterward ([Leviticus 23:10](https://ref.ly/Lev23:10)).
2. **Pentecost**, the Feast of Weeks, is celebrated 50 days after Passover. It is a joyful festival connected with the firstfruits of the wheat harvest ([Exodus 34:22](https://ref.ly/Exod34:22); [Leviticus 23:15–17](https://ref.ly/Lev23:15-Lev23:17)).
3. **New Year’s Day** comes next and marks the beginning of Tishri. According to the rabbis, the first day of Tishri was the day when the Lord created the world. This day is called Rosh Hashanah, which means “head of the year.”
4. **Yom Kippur**, the Day of Atonement, is observed on the tenth day of Tishri. Atonement refers to how God deals with sin and restores people to a right relationship with him. Yom Kippur is the most serious day in Israel’s religious calendar. It is known as “the Sabbath of Sabbaths.” The Bible gives detailed instructions for observing this day ([Leviticus 16](https://ref.ly/Lev16:1-Lev16:34)).
5. **Succoth**, the Feast of Tabernacles, takes place from the 15th through the 22nd day of Tishri. It is mainly a harvest festival. It celebrates the crops gathered in September and October. The apostle John referred to it simply as “the feast” ([John 7:37](https://ref.ly/John7:37)). The Feast of Tabernacles is also called the Feast of Booths or Shelters. It remembers how God cared for his people during Israel’s 40 years in the wilderness ([Leviticus 23:39–43](https://ref.ly/Lev23:39-Lev23:43)).
6. **Hanukkah**, the Feast of Dedication, began later in Jewish history. It remembers Judas Maccabeus’s decisive victory over Antiochus Epiphanes and the Syrians around 164 BC. Malachi, the last Old Testament prophet, lived long before this event. For this reason, tradition alone guides how the festival is celebrated. Hanukkah begins on the 25th day of Chislev and continues for seven more days. It is marked by joyful celebrations.
7. **Purim** is the final sacred festival of the year. It takes place on the 14th and 15th days of Adar. The festival began in ancient Persia. It remembers how Mordecai and Esther helped save the Jews by stopping Haman’s plan to destroy them ([Esther 9](https://ref.ly/Esth9:1-Esth9:32)).

Conclusion
----------

Ancient sundials and modern clocks measure the passing of minutes and hours. In the same way, a calendar measures the passing of days, weeks, months, years, and even centuries.

Farming, business, and government all need a clear and consistent way to measure longer periods of time. Such a system is also important for historians. It also helps religious communities celebrate special days at the same time.

The modern Gregorian calendar developed through a combination of astronomy, history, and religious tradition. For Christians, the calendar also has religious meaning because the Bible contrasts God’s eternal nature with the shortness of human life ([Psalm 90](https://ref.ly/Ps90:1-Ps90:17)). 

The psalmist asked God to “teach us to number our days, that we may present a heart of wisdom” ([Psalm 90:12](https://ref.ly/Ps90:12)).

*See also* Astrology; Astronomy; Day; Feasts and Festivals of Israel; Jubilee Year; Moon; Night; Sun.

## Caligula (id: 4394)

CALIGULA
========

Caligula was the nickname of Gaius Julius Caesar, who became emperor of Rome at age 25, after the death of Emperor Tiberius. Caligula was emperor of Rome from AD 37 to 41\. 

Early Life and Name
-------------------

Gaius was the son of Germanicus, a respected Roman military leader. The emperor Augustus told Tiberius to adopt Gaius and name him as the next emperor.

As a child, Gaius traveled with his father along the Rhine River in what is now Germany. Soldiers called him *Caligula*, which means “Little Boot,” because he wore small soldier’s boots. The name stayed with him.

Caligula Becomes Emperor of Rome
--------------------------------

When Caligula became emperor, he first tried to win the people's support. He freed prisoners and allowed exiled people to return. But he quickly used up all the money in the Roman treasury. He had to create new taxes, and the people grew unhappy with him.

Six months after becoming emperor, Caligula became very sick. After this illness, his behavior changed. He began to act strangely. He once made his horse a consul (a top government leader). He also insulted many people and killed others without reason.

Conflict with the Jews
----------------------

Caligula believed the Jewish people in Jamnia had insulted him. (Jamnia was a town in Judea, near the Mediterranean Sea.) In anger, he ordered a statue of himself to be placed in the Jerusalem temple.

The Jewish people were very upset. A major revolt was only stopped because the governor of Syria, Petronius, delayed carrying out the order. Not long after this, Caligula was killed by one of the men he had mistreated.

One of Caligula’s first acts as emperor was to make Herod Agrippa I king over a region near Galilee ([Acts 12](https://ref.ly/Acts12:1-Acts12:13)). Caligula and Agrippa had been friends in Rome before gaining power. While Caligula ruled badly, Agrippa was a wise and well\-liked king.

Like many eastern rulers, both Caligula and Agrippa claimed to be gods. Caligula even said he was equal to Jupiter, the chief Roman god. But the Roman Senate never agreed to this claim.

*See also* Caesars, The.

* **Associated Articles:** The Caesars (ID: `4380@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

## Callisthenes (id: 4396)

CALLISTHENES
============

Callisthenes was a Syrian in the army of General Nicanor. He set fire to the temple gates during the persecution of the Jews by Antiochus Epiphanes ([2 Maccabees 8:33](https://ref.ly/2Macc8:33)). In 165 BC, Nicanor’s army was defeated. After the battle, the Jews punished Callisthenes and others who had burned the temple gates. They were burned to death.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Maccabees 8:33


## Calneh (id: 4397)

CALNEH
======

1. A city in Babylon ([Genesis 10:10](https://ref.ly/Gen10:10)).
2. A city identified as Kullani (Kullan Koy), about 32\.2 kilometers (20 miles) north of Aleppo in northern Syria. [Isaiah 10:9](https://ref.ly/Isa10:9) supports this northern location by linking Calno with Carchemish, about 80\.5 kilometers (50 miles) to the northeast. [Amos 6:2](https://ref.ly/Amos6:2) may also support it by listing places from north to south: Calneh, then Hamath, and then Gath.

    Calneh in [Ezekiel 27:23](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:23) may refer to the same general area and may be another form of the name Calneh. Kullani was captured by Tiglath\-pileser III, king of Assyria, around 741 BC.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 10:10; Isaiah 10:9; Ezekiel 27:23; Amos 6:2

## Camel (id: 4401)

CAMEL
=====

A large animal that lives in desert areas. Camels can go for many days without drinking water. People have trained camels to be work animals. They use camels for travel and to carry heavy loads across the Middle East

The camel (Camelus dromedarius) has special features that help it live in the desert. It has been called the "ship of the desert" because, like ships that carry cargo across oceans, camels carry heavy loads across vast desert areas. Its feet have thick elastic pads of fibrous tissue that allow it to walk on hot desert sands. It can go without water for long periods and can live on plants growing on the salty sand. The camel can close its pinched nostrils to keep out sand in violent sandstorms.

Camels are used for transporting both goods and people. A person riding a camel can cover from 96\.5 to 121 kilometers (60 to 75 miles) in a day. A camel can carry a load weighing 272 kilograms (600 pounds) or more. Camels were vital in the spice trade ([Genesis 37:25](https://ref.ly/Gen37:25)). They traveled in camel trains between Arabia, Egypt, and Assyria. They were also ridden in time of war ([Judges 6:5](https://ref.ly/Judg6:5)). Farmers can even hitch a camel to a plow in areas where they farm.

The hair shed by camels during the early spring is kept and used in making cloth and tents. One camel can make as much as 4\.5 kilograms (ten pounds) of hair. A rough cloak of camel’s hair, as worn by John the Baptist ([Matthew 3:4](https://ref.ly/Matt3:4)), is still worn by people who live in the desert. A camel’s hair garment was also the sign of a prophet ([Zechariah 13:4](https://ref.ly/Zech13:4)).

There are two types of one\-humped camels: the slow, burden\-bearing camel from [Genesis 37:25](https://ref.ly/Gen37:25) and the fast racing camel mentioned in [1 Samuel 30:17](https://ref.ly/1Sam30:17). The racing camel can be up to 2\.1 meters (seven feet) tall and 2\.7 meters (nine feet) long. Its stomach holds 14\.2 to 28\.4 liters (15 to 30 quarts) of liquid. This camel can go without water for five days in summer or 25 days in winter. Its hump stores fat, allowing it to survive on little food during desert trips.

Another species of camel lives in the holy land, the Bactrian camel *(Camelus bactrianus)*. It has two humps. It is heavier, bigger, and has longer hair than the one\-humped camel and is slower than the fast racing camel. [Isaiah 21:7](https://ref.ly/Isa21:7) may refer to the Bactrian camel. Both kinds of camel are referred to in [Esther 8:10](https://ref.ly/Esth8:10). In ancient times, camels were as important as sheep, cattle, and donkeys. The Bible mentions camels 66 times. One third of these references list camels alongside other animals.

Camels chew the cud, but do not have cloven hooves. So, they were unclean, forbidden by the Israelites to eat ([Leviticus 11:4](https://ref.ly/Lev11:4); [Deuteronomy 14:7](https://ref.ly/Deut14:7)). They are eaten by Arabs, however, who also drink their milk (see [Genesis 32:15](https://ref.ly/Gen32:15)).

According to the Bible, Abraham had camels when he traveled to Egypt ([Genesis 12:16](https://ref.ly/Gen12:16)). At first, Job had 3,000 camels, and after his recovery, 6,000 ([Job 1:3](https://ref.ly/Job1:3); [42:12](https://ref.ly/Job42:12)). Most people began using camels around 1000 BC ([Judges 6:5](https://ref.ly/Judg6:5)). But, Sumerian texts from the Old Babylonian period show people had trained camels even earlier. Archaeologists have found camel bones and figurines at various eastern sites dating from well before 1200 BC.

*See also* Travel.

* **Associated Articles:** Animals (ID: `3614@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Travel (ID: `8915@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`)

## Cana (id: 4406)

CANA
====

Cana was a town in Galilee where Jesus performed his first miracle. At a wedding feast, he changed water into wine ([John 2:1, 11](https://ref.ly/John2:1,John2:11)). Jesus later returned to Cana. While there, he told a royal official that his seriously ill son in Capernaum would live ([John 4:46](https://ref.ly/John4:46)). Cana was also the home of Nathanael, one of the disciples of Jesus ([John 21:2](https://ref.ly/John21:2)).

During the first Jewish rebellion, the Romans destroyed Jerusalem in AD 70\. Cana became the center of defense for Galilee against the Romans. After the destruction of Jerusalem and the temple, the town became home to the priestly family of Eliashib. John's Gospel calls it "Cana of Galilee." This distinguishes it from the Kanah near the Phoenician city of Tyre ([Joshua 19:28](https://ref.ly/Josh19:28)). 

The traditional location of Cana is Kefar Kana, about 6\.4 kilometers (four miles) east of Nazareth on the main road to Tiberias. Christians have honored this site since the Byzantine and medieval periods. 

However, most modern scholars identify Cana with Khirbet Kana. This ruined site lies about 12\.9 kilometers (eight miles) north of Nazareth, on the northern edge of the Battuf Plain. Local Arabs still call it Cana of Galilee. Archaeologists have found pottery there from the period of the Hebrew monarchy, about 900–600 BC. They have also found pottery from the Hellenistic, Roman, Arab, and Crusader periods.

* **Associated Passages:** Joshua 19:28; John 2:1; John 2:11; John 4:46; John 21:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cana (ID: `place:Cana`)

## Canaan, Canaanite (id: 4404)

CANAAN, CANAANITE
=================

Palestinian territory (the Promised Land) west of the Jordan River, settled by the Israelites at the time of Joshua’s leadership. Portions of southern Syria were also frequently considered part of Canaanite territory, the northern borders of which were never clearly defined. The pre\-Israelite peoples of western Palestine, excluding northern Syria and such places as Ugarit (Ras Shamra) on the Mediterranean coast of Syria, carried the broad designation of Canaanites.

Preview

•Land and People

•Language

•Literature

•History

•Religion

•Influence on Israel

Land and People
---------------

In the “table of nations” ([Gn 10:15–19](https://ref.ly/Gen10:15-Gen10:19)), Noah’s grandson Canaan was progenitor of 11 groups that lived in the area of Syria and Palestine. The first six evidently occupied territory at or south of Sidon, whereas the others lived farther north. The northerners mostly settled on the edge of the coastal plain; in the south, settlement spread eastward to the upland areas. OT references specifically placed the Canaanites in western Palestine’s valleys and coastal areas; the upland country was occupied by Amorites and other peoples ([Nm 13:29](https://ref.ly/Num13:29); [Jos 5:1](https://ref.ly/Josh5:1); [7:9](https://ref.ly/Josh7:9); [Jgs 1:27–36](https://ref.ly/Judg1:27-Judg1:36)).

One of the earliest known references to the people of Canaan is in a tablet from Mari (15th century BC), in which a military officer reported his surveillance of “thieves and Canaanites.” The Canaanites were listed as a group on the Memphis Stele (inscribed column) of the Egyptian pharaoh Amenophis II (c. 1440 BC). The land of Canaan was mentioned in a 15th\-century inscription of Idrimi, king of Aleppo (west of Ugarit), who fled to the Canaanite seaport of Ammiya and then became ruler of Alalakh (north of Ugarit). During the Amarna Age (15th–14th centuries BC), Palestine was politically dominated by Egypt, according to the Egyptian Amarna tablets.

Just as “Canaan” designated the whole western Palestinian area, so “Canaanite” described its pre\-Israelite inhabitants without specifying race. Among the peoples who lived in Palestine, the Amorites first appeared in the second millennium BC as immigrants from Mesopotamia.

Several OT references seem to equate Amorite territory and the land of Canaan ([Gn 12:5–6](https://ref.ly/Gen12:5-Gen12:6); [15:18–21](https://ref.ly/Gen15:18-Gen15:21); [48:22](https://ref.ly/Gen48:22)), a tradition reflected in the 18th\-century BC Alalakh tablets, which depicted “Amurru” as part of Syria\-Palestine. Tablets from Mari from about the same period speak of an Amorite ruler of Hazor in northern Palestine. The Tell el\-Amarna texts (14th–13th centuries BC) indicate that the Amurru kingdom of the Lebanon region was monopolizing coastal trade and commerce; therefore, references to the two peoples (Amorites and Canaanites) together in Moses’ time and throughout the late Bronze Age (c. 1550–1200 BC) are not surprising.

At the end of that period, the “Sea Peoples” (largely Philistines) destroyed the Hittite Empire, and in the time of Ramses III (c. 1180 BC) occupied western Palestine. The Israelite conquest of Palestine broke the power of many Canaanite and Amorite city\-states, while the rise of a Philistine confederacy on the southern Palestinian coast restricted further the range of specifically Canaanite territory. From the beginning of the Iron Age the cultural heirs of the Canaanites were the Phoenicians, centered in the city\-states of Tyre and Sidon, who themselves liked to be known as Canaanites (cf. [Mt 15:21–22](https://ref.ly/Matt15:21-Matt15:22); [Mk 7:24–26](https://ref.ly/Mark7:24-Mark7:26)).

Language
--------

The various groups that inhabited western Palestine in the pre\-Israelite period probably spoke related dialects of the Northwest Semitic linguistic family. The large territory covered by those peoples and the possible influence of Amorite, Hurrian, and Ugaritic languages complicate modern theories about what is properly meant by “Canaanite” as a language.

Literature
----------

As with language, it is difficult to be specific about Canaanite literature. One clear fact is that our own alphabet originated in middle Bronze Age Canaan. Before that time, writing was either pictographic (words or ideas represented by pictures), cuneiform (wedge\-shaped impressions in soft clay representing syllables and whole words), or hieroglyphic (Egyptian pictorial writing). Alphabetical writing was passed on through the Hebrews and Phoenicians to the Greeks, who gave our present alphabet its classical form.

Until 1929 little Canaanite literature was known, but with the discoveries at Ugarit a large body of literary material came to light. The discoveries included portions of an epic poem about the god Baal and his consort Anath (possibly from c. 2000 BC), a legend about a royal personage named Aqhat (from c. 1800 BC), the legendary activities of King Keret (written c. 1500 BC), and fragmentary religious, medical, and administrative material.

History
-------

Archaeological evidence shows that western Palestine was occupied as far back as the Old Stone Age. Mesolithic, Neolithic, and Chalcolithic deposits have also been found at several sites. It is possible that Semitic\-speaking peoples inhabited places such as Jericho, Megiddo, and Byblos around 3000 BC. Discoveries at Tell Mardikh (Ebla) show that a vigorous Canaanite Empire existed in Syria about 2500 BC, and there is no doubt that both Amorite and Canaanite peoples were firmly settled in Syria and Palestine by 2000 BC. The best evidence for Canaanite occupation of western Palestine has come from the middle and late Bronze Age (c. 1950–1200 BC), when the land was dotted with Canaanite and Amorite city\-states.

The Egyptians made periodic forays into Palestine during their 5th and 6th dynasties; in the 13th dynasty (second millennium BC) they controlled much of Syria\-Palestine both politically and economically.

Canaanite contacts with Mesopotamia from about 2000 BC are indicated in texts discovered at Mari and Ugarit. Evidently Amorites, Hurrians, early Assyrians, and other peoples periodically migrated to Canaan, bringing with them a diversity of political and social forms. By the late 16th century BC, most of the small Canaanite kingdoms were firmly under Egyptian control. Within two centuries the most northerly ones were subject to Hittite political influence.

Canaanite history is further complicated by the activities of the Hyksos people between about 1800 and 1500 BC. Of mixed Asiatic origin, the Hyksos owed much of their political influence to their military use of iron\-fitted chariots and the compound Asiatic bow. From Canaanite locations like Hazor and Jericho, they invaded Egypt and established control there from about 1776 to about 1570 BC. When they were expelled at the start of Egypt’s New Kingdom (1570–1100 BC), they retreated to fortified sites in southern Canaan.

Egyptian control over western Palestine had disappeared by the time of the Israelite conquest of Canaan; Joshua met predominantly Canaanite and Amorite opposition. The Israelite occupation of Canaan was aided by the state of decay into which the small Palestinian kingdoms had fallen. With the destruction of Hittite culture by the Sea Peoples and their occupation of the northern and coastal regions, the traditional city\-states collapsed. From about 1100 BC, Canaanite culture was restricted to Tyre, Sidon, and a few other places.

Religion
--------

Before the Ugaritic discoveries, little was known about Canaanite religion apart from OT references to it. From what is now known of Canaanite culture, the head of the Canaanite list of gods was a shadowy personage named El, who was worshiped as the “father of man.” His consorts were Athirat, known to the Israelites as Asherah, Astarte, and Baaltis. El had a son, Baal, a fertility god described in myths as the lord of rain and storm. Baal succeeded his father as head of the pantheon (list of gods) and supposedly resided in the distant northern heavens. A monument found at Ugarit represented him carrying a thunderbolt at his left side and a mace in his right hand.

Many small terra\-cotta figurines with exaggerated secondary sexual characteristics, representing one or the other of the female deities, have been recovered from middle and late Bronze Age sites in western Palestine. A center devoted to the Anath cult, excavated at Byblos in Phoenicia, was evidently notorious for religious prostitution and sexual fertility rites; many naked female figures were found there. Other Canaanite cult objects included a sacred pillar of some sort *(*massebah*)* and a wooden image *(*asherah*),* probably of the goddess herself.

In the Amarna Age, Canaanite orgiastic religion was especially influential in the Near East; it infiltrated to some extent even the conservative religions of Egypt and Babylonia. Four principal festivals associated with agriculture seem to have been celebrated by the Canaanites, invariably occasions of revelry, drunkenness, and sexual excess. Canaanite religion was evidently the most sexually depraved of any in the ancient world.

Influence on Israel
-------------------

Israelite morality, as defined by the covenant laws of Mt Sinai, was very different from the cultic traditions of Canaanite life. Hebrew ethical monotheism was in many ways opposite to the depraved polytheistic nature worship of Canaanite religion. It was clear that the two systems could not coexist. Hence the law contained strict instructions that the Canaanites and their ways were to be eliminated from the Promised Land ([Ex 23:24](https://ref.ly/Exod23:24); [34:13–16](https://ref.ly/Exod34:13-Exod34:16); [Dt 7:1–5](https://ref.ly/Deut7:1-Deut7:5)) and that the Hebrews were to remain separate from Canaanite religion in loyalty to God’s covenant. That was far from easy, if only because both peoples spoke closely related dialects and therefore used similar expressions of speech. Further, the invading Israelites under Joshua found that the Canaanites were superior to them in building stone structures and in making metal tools, implements, and weapons. The Hebrews, at a disadvantage, must have faced the prospect of requiring technical help from the Canaanites. In the time of King Solomon, Canaanites from Phoenicia were enlisted to design and construct the temple of the Lord in Jerusalem. A superficial resemblance between some aspects of Canaanite and Hebrew religion, such as peace offerings and certain divine titles, also made it difficult to maintain Israel’s cultural distinctiveness.

Except for the “ban” imposed at Jericho, the Israelites were able to use Canaanite equipment captured in battle. Hence their determination to destroy all traces of the Canaanites, including their corrupt religion, was gradually weakened. By the time of King Ahab, when the worship of the Tyrian Baal was firmly entrenched in the northern kingdom of Israel, the Hebrews were in serious danger of losing their spiritual and theological distinctiveness. Their priests, who should have played a major part in maintaining the uniqueness of the covenant faith, often lapsed into Canaanite ways, emulating the immorality of their pagan neighbors and encouraging the Israelite people to do likewise (cf. [1 Sm 2:22](https://ref.ly/1Sam2:22)).

As a result, Hebrew prophets proclaimed that their nation, which had almost completely succumbed to Canaanite blandishments, would have to be purified by exile before a renewed faith could become a possibility for Israel.

*See also* Canaanite Deities and Religion; Israel, History of; Palestine.

## Canaanite Gods and Religion (id: 4405)

CANAANITE GODS AND RELIGION
===========================

Studying the religion of the Canaanites helps us understand the religion of ancient Israel. The Canaanites worshiped many gods. God gave Israel its beliefs and religious practices, but the Israelites lived among peoples who followed other religions. These religions influenced them.

To understand Israel's faith in one God, it is important to understand this setting. The Israelites lived among peoples who worshiped many gods. These beliefs challenged Israel's faithfulness and unity as a nation.

Contact among the many religions of the ancient Near East caused tension. It also led to syncretism (the mixing of religious beliefs and practices). The Arameans and Philistines who settled in Canaan adopted many Canaanite practices. In the same way, when the Amorites moved into Mesopotamia, they accepted many parts of Sumerian religion.

The Hebrews, however, followed a different path. They worshiped one God, who was unique and ruled over all creation. Their God required them to worship him alone. This belief was very different from the other religions of that time.

The Ugaritic Texts
------------------

Until the early 20th century, most knowledge about Canaanite religion came from the Bible. In 1928, many clay tablets were discovered at Ras Shamra, the site of the ancient Syrian city of Ugarit. These tablets gave scholars much new information about Canaanite religious life.

Most of the tablets were written in a cuneiform alphabet, a writing system made with wedge\-shaped marks. They used a previously unknown Northwest Semitic language that was similar to Hebrew, Aramaic, and Arabic. These documents are often called the Ugaritic texts or the Ras Shamra tablets.

The discovery of these texts gave scholars new ways to understand Canaanite religion. The texts included important stories about the gods. They explained the names and roles of the gods and also gave much information about Canaanite society.

Canaanite gods and goddesses had two important features: 

1. Their personalities and roles could change greatly.
2. The meanings and origins of their names were often easy to identify.

Together with the nature of the stories about these gods, these features show that Canaanite religion was relatively simple and early in its development.

El, Chief of the Gods
---------------------

The general Canaanite word for “god” probably meant “the strong or powerful one.” The chief god in the pantheon (the group of gods) was El, whose name meant “the mighty one.”

El was a distant and mysterious figure. He was said to live far from Canaan “at the source of the two rivers,” a place understood as paradise. He apparently had three wives who were also his sisters: 

* Astarte,
* Athirat (Asherah, also called Elat), and
* Anath.

El led a divine council, a group of gods who were described as his children. Although the stories say that he killed his own son, he was also called Lutpan, meaning “the kindly one.” He was described as an old man with white hair and a beard.

Baal, the Storm God
-------------------

Baal, the great storm god, was called king of the gods. He was the central figure in the Canaanite pantheon and was much more important in practice than El. Baal served as El’s chief helper and later removed him from power.

The name “Baal” means “lord” and could be used for different gods. Over time, however, the ancient Semitic storm god Hadad became known especially as Baal. Hadad was called the “lord of heaven,” the “one who prevails,” and the “exalted lord of the earth.” He was said to rule over everything else, and his kingdom was described as lasting through all generations.

Baal was also believed to give fertility, meaning the ability of land, animals, and people to produce life. When he died, plants stopped growing and living creatures stopped reproducing. He was also described as a god of justice who brought fear to those who did evil.

Baal was called the “son of Dagon.” Dagon, meaning “fish,” was the chief god of Ashdod (compare [1 Samuel 5:1–7](https://ref.ly/1Sam5:1-1Sam5:7)).

The Gods and the Seasons
------------------------

The Canaanites explained nature by reference to their gods. Each god represented some force of nature. The moon, sun, important stars, and visible planets were each considered a god or goddess. Baal, seen as god of the thunderstorm, personified the power of all nature.

The Canaanites believed that the actions of their gods explained the changing seasons. The dry season lasted from April until the end of October. They believed this was the time when Baal was dead after losing his battle with Mot each spring. At Ras Shamra, other figures called “the devourers” had a role similar to Mot.

The Canaanites believed that Baal, the god connected with rain and plant growth, returned to life near the end of October. His return marked the beginning of the autumn rains. These rains continued at different times until the following April.

The Canaanites also believed that the land became fertile again each year because Baal and Anath had sexual relations. They copied the sexual actions they believed their chief gods performed as part of their religious practices. For this reason, sexual activity was an important part of Canaanite religious worship.

The Three Main Canaanite Goddesses
----------------------------------

The three main goddesses were:

* Athtarat
* Anath
* Athirat

They had a complex set of relationships. Athtarat was also called Astarte or Ashtaroth in the Old Testament ([Deuteronomy 1:4](https://ref.ly/Deut1:4); [Judges 2:13](https://ref.ly/Judg2:13), “Ashtoreths” in the Berean Standard Bible). Astarte was identified with Ashtar, or Venus, the evening star.

Anath’s original role is uncertain. Her name appears in the Old Testament in the name of the town Anathoth. She is also connected with Shamgar, who is called “son of Anath.”

Athirat was called Asherah in the Old Testament. She was mainly a goddess of the sea and the wife of El. She was also called Elat, the feminine form of the name El.

### The Goddesses, Sex, and War

All three goddesses were mainly connected with sex and war. Their main role was to have sexual relations with Baal in a yearly cycle. Yet the stories still described them as “virgins.” They were called “the great goddesses who conceive but do not bear.”

The goddesses were also connected with sacred prostitution and were called “the holy ones.” Idols that represented them were often shown without clothing. Some also had exaggerated sexual features. Scholars still debate when and how early religious prostitution was practiced. There is no doubt, however, that both men and women served as temple prostitutes in Canaanite religious worship.

The fertility goddesses were also connected with war. In the Baal Epic from Ugarit, Anath is described as having a violent desire for blood. Egyptian sources from the New Kingdom period describe Astarte as a fierce warrior who rode in battle. She was shown without clothing and carried a shield and spear.

### Asherah and Places of Worship

In the King James Version, the name Asherah was translated as “grove.” This followed the Septuagint, a Greek translation of the Old Testament from the third century BC. Asherah seems to have been represented by a wooden object used in worship. This object was placed at “high places,” or worship sites on elevated ground, beside incense altars and stone pillars.

Sacrifices and Canaanite Worship
--------------------------------

The Canaanites often struggled to survive. This may have led them to worship gods they believed could provide food and other material needs. They believed that if the gods and goddesses were pleased with their worship, they would receive a good harvest.

Canaanite worship often took place at a religious shrine or “high place.” People offered sacrifices there. Archaeological evidence shows that people sacrificed animals of different sizes at large temple sites such as Beth\-shan.

The city of Beth\-shan received its name from the temple there. The word *beth* means “temple,” and Shan was the patron god, or special god, of the city.

Human sacrifice also became part of religious practice in Canaan. [Second Kings 3:27](https://ref.ly/2Kgs3:27) tells about Mesha, the king of Moab. After he was defeated by a group of kings, he sacrificed his son as a burnt offering to his god, Chemosh.

*See also* Canaan, Canaanite; Gods and Goddesses; Idols, Idolatry.

* **Associated Passages:** Deuteronomy 1:4; Judges 2:13; 2 Kings 3:27; 1 Samuel 5:1-7

## Candace (id: 4407)

CANDACE
=======

A title given to ancient Ethiopian queens. Philip, a leader in the early church, met and baptized an Ethiopian eunuch who was a minister under Candace, queen of the Ethiopians ([Acts 8:27](https://ref.ly/Acts8:27)). That particular Candace ruled over Nubia (modern Sudan) from AD 25 to 41\. Her name was probably Amanitere.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 8:27


## Candle, Candlestick (id: 4408)

CANDLE, CANDLESTICK
===================

The words candle and candlestick appear in older Bible versions like the King James Version. But these words are better translated as lamp and lampstand.

Candles made of wax with a wick like we use today did not exist in Bible times. People used small clay or metal lamps filled with oil. A piece of cloth served as the wick.

*See* Lamp, Lampstand.

* **Associated Articles:** Lamp, Lampstand (ID: `6675@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Cane, Sweet Cane (id: 4409)

CANE, SWEET CANE
================

A tall grass plant grown for its sweet sap. The sap is used to make sugar. Two types of sugarcane are thought to have grown naturally in Israel and the surrounding areas. One type, *Saccharum sara*, is known to grow only in Lebanon. The other native type is *Saccharum biflorum*, which grows along ditches and streams. It grows from Syria and Lebanon through Israel and the surrounding areas, and south to Stony Arabia and the Sinai. This may be the wild cane familiar to the Jewish people.

Most experts, however, believe that the "sweet cane" mentioned in [Isaiah 43:24](https://ref.ly/Isa43:24) was the true sugarcane (*Saccharum officinarum*). This plant is believed to have come from the tropical areas of the eastern part of the world. People have grown this plant since ancient times, and it is not known to grow wild anywhere today. It is a tall, thick perennial grass that looks similar to maize (corn). It has many jointed stems and a large, feathery cluster of flowers at the top.

*See also* Aromatic Cane.

* **Associated Articles:** Aromatic Cane (ID: `3760@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Plants (ID: `7761@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

## Cankerworm (id: 4410)

CANKERWORM
==========

Cankerworm is a word used in the King James Version to translate a Hebrew word that means "locust." Locusts are insects that eat plants and crops. Large groups of locusts can cause great damage to farmland.

*See* Locusts.

## Canon of the Bible (id: 4224)

CANON OF THE BIBLE
==================

A biblical canon is a collection of books that a Jewish or Christian community accepts as Scripture. These books are authoritative for faith and teaching.

The word “canon” translates a Greek word and a Hebrew word that mean “rule” or “measuring rod.” A canon is a standard. People use it to decide which books belong in Scripture.

After the fourth century AD, many Christians recognized 66 books as Scripture. These included 27 books in the New Testament and 39 books in the Old Testament. In the same way that the works of Plato, Aristotle, and Homer became an accepted collection of Greek literature, the New Testament books became the accepted collection of Christian writings.

We do not know the exact standards used to select the books of the Jewish Scriptures, often called the Hebrew Bible and known to Christians as the Old Testament. The Jewish people clearly valued these books for their faith, worship, and life.

Early Christian writers said that “apostolicity” was important when selecting New Testament books. Apostolicity means that a book had a close connection to the apostles or their teaching. Local churches collected and preserved these books. They used them in worship and for authoritative guidance in Christian life. The Old Testament books were collected and preserved in a similar way.

The canon developed over time. No single event established it. The process took several hundred years and did not end at the same time in every part of the Roman Empire.

At first, local churches had their own collections of books. Over time, Christians compared these collections, and a more widely accepted canon developed. Most churches still use this canon today. However, some Eastern churches accept a slightly smaller New Testament than churches in the West.

Both Judaism and Christianity believe that God’s Spirit worked in a special, guiding way in the writing and preservation of his word.

*See* Old Testament Canon; New Testament Canon. 

* **Associated Passages:** Deuteronomy 4:2; Deuteronomy 25:4; Deuteronomy 31:26; Psalms 4:4; Matthew 22:14; Matthew 26:28; Mark 12:26; Luke 10:7; Luke 24:44; John 14:26; John 16:13; 1 Corinthians 2:13; 1 Corinthians 11:25; 1 Corinthians 14:37; Ephesians 2:12; Ephesians 4:11; Ephesians 4:26; Colossians 4:16; 1 Thessalonians 2:13; 1 Thessalonians 5:27; 1 Timothy 5:18; Hebrews 9:1; Revelation 1:3; Jeremiah 31:31-34; Hebrews 8:7-13; Hebrews 9:15-22; 2 Peter 3:15-16; Luke 1:1-80; Luke 2:1-52; 1 Corinthians 14:29-30
