## Canticles (id: 4414)

CANTICLES
=========

Another title for the biblical book, Song of Songs (or Song of Solomon). The name comes from the Latin name of the book, *Canticum Canticorum* (which means "Song of Songs")*.*

*See* Song of Songs.

* **Associated Articles:** Song of Songs (ID: `8568@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Caper Plant (id: 4416)

CAPER PLANT
===========

A low, spreading plant whose fruit was used to stimulate appetite. The caper (*Capparis sincula*) is a spiny, trailing shrub that grows in the Mediterranean region. [Ecclesiastes 12:5](https://ref.ly/Eccl12:5) mentions the caper berry.

The common caper or caper berry grows abundantly in Syria, Lebanon, Israel and the surrounding areas, and in the mountain valleys of Sinai. The plant sometimes grows upright but more often spreads weakly over the ground like a vine, covering rocks, ruins, and old walls similar to ivy. The young flower buds, pickled in vinegar, were used by ancient people as a condiment for meat. The berries were also used in cooking.

* **Associated Articles:** Plants (ID: `7761@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Capernaum (id: 4415)

CAPERNAUM
=========

Capernaum was a city in Galilee where Jesus based his work and taught regularly. This city is only mentioned in the Gospels. It was on the northwest side of the Sea of Galilee (also called Lake of Gennesaret). Capernaum means “village of Nahum.” The name might refer to Nahum, who wrote a book in the Old Testament, or it could be named after a different person called Nahum.

Where Was Capernaum Located?
----------------------------

The Gospel of Matthew says Capernaum was located near the Sea of Galilee, in the area of Zebulun and Naphtali ([Matthew 4:13](https://ref.ly/Matt4:13)). The west shore of the lake was where the tribe of Naphtali lived. 

Two stories about people in Capernaum show it was near the border of the Jordan River and the political border. The story of the centurion suggests there was a small group of about 100 soldiers in this border town ([Matthew 8:5](https://ref.ly/Matt8:5); [Luke 7:2](https://ref.ly/Luke7:2)). In another story, Jesus asks Levi to leave his job collecting taxes. It also shows the town was on a border ([Matthew 9:9](https://ref.ly/Matt9:9); [Mark 2:14](https://ref.ly/Mark2:14); [Luke 5:27](https://ref.ly/Luke5:27)).

Ancient Jewish writings tell us that Capernaum was located at a place called Tell Hum. We know Capernaum existed in the year AD 110 because it was referred to in Midrash Kohelet 1\.8 and 7\.26 as "Kaphar nahum.” This shows that Capernaum still existed in the second century. 

Capernaum is not mentioned again until 1160, when Benjamin of Tudela talks about “Capharnaum which is Kaphar Nahum.” The Franciscans (a religious order of priests) now own the site. They have found many coins from the time at Tell Hum. They also found ruins of a Jewish place of worship (called a synagogue) that was probably built around AD 300\. 

Recent digging at Tell Hum has proven this was definitely where ancient Capernaum stood. Archaeologists found an old house under a building that Jewish Christians used for meetings in the fourth century. This house had been used as a meeting place for early Christians since around the first half of the second century. Based on ancient writings and markings found on stones, many scholars think this was Peter's house.

Capernaum in the New Testament
------------------------------

Capernaum was an important city where Roman soldiers were stationed. After the people of Nazareth rejected Jesus, he came to live in Capernaum ([Matthew 9:1](https://ref.ly/Matt9:1)). Capernaum was his home and Jesus did many miracles ([Mark 2:1](https://ref.ly/Mark2:1); [1:34](https://ref.ly/Mark1:34)). 

* He healed the centurion’s servant ([Matthew 8:5](https://ref.ly/Matt8:5)).
* He healed Peter’s mother\-in\-law ([Mark 1:31](https://ref.ly/Mark1:31)).
* He healed a man with an unclean spirit ([Mark 1:23](https://ref.ly/Mark1:23); [Luke 4:33](https://ref.ly/Luke4:33)).

Even though Jesus did a lot of good things in Capernaum, he said bad things would happen to the city because the people did not change their ways: “And you, Capernaum, will you be lifted up to heaven? No, you will be brought down to Hades” ([Matthew 11:23](https://ref.ly/Matt11:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Matthew 4:13; Matthew 8:5; Matthew 9:1; Matthew 9:9; Matthew 11:23; Mark 1:23; Mark 1:31; Mark 1:34; Mark 2:1; Mark 2:14; Luke 4:33; Luke 5:27; Luke 7:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Capernaum (ID: `place:Capernaum`)

## Caphar-salama (id: 4417)

CAPHAR\-SALAMA
==============

A village where Judas Maccabeus fought the Syrian commander Nicanor ([1 Maccabees 7:31](https://ref.ly/1Macc7:31)). After about 500 of the men of Nicanor died, the fugitives took refuge in the “city of David.” This means Caphar\-salama must have been near Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Maccabees 7:31


## Caphtor, Caphtorites, Caphtorim (id: 4418)

CAPHTOR, CAPHTORITES, CAPHTORIM
===============================

Caphtor is a place\-name, while Caphtorites and Caphtorim are names for the people connected with that place.

In the table of nations, the Capthorites are listed among the descendants of Ham and as descendants of Egypt ([Genesis 10:13–14](https://ref.ly/Gen10:13-Gen10:14); [1 Chronicles 1:12](https://ref.ly/1Chr1:12)). The text lists the Casluhites as the ancestors of the Philistines. However, the prophets describe the Philistines as people who came from Caphtor ([Jeremiah 47:4](https://ref.ly/Jer47:4); see [Amos 9:7](https://ref.ly/Amos9:7)). 

Because of this difference, some translations move part of [Genesis 10:14](https://ref.ly/Gen10:14) and translate the text as, “Caphtorites, from whom the Philistines were descended” (New English Bible). Others suggest that the Philistines first came from the Casluhim but later settled in areas mainly associated with the Caphtorites.

Caphtor is referred to as *Kaptara* in Accadian, *kptr* in Ugaritic, and *Keftiu* in Egyptian. These references are to be dated from as early as 2200 BC down to about 1200 BC. The Egyptian sources are especially helpful. They identify Caphtor as Crete. However, a Jewish tradition connects the Caphtorites with Cappadocia, and the Septuagint uses “Cappadocia” instead of “Caphtor.” Because of this, some scholars identify Caphtor with a coastal area of Asia Minor or with the island of Carpathos. By the 13th century BC, the name “Caphtor” may have referred more broadly to the Aegean region from which the Philistines came.

The Caphtorites are also described as people who invaded the region near Gaza. They drove out the Avvites and settled in their land ([Deuteronomy 2:23](https://ref.ly/Deut2:23)). The Caphtorites seem to have been firmly established around Gaza before Israel crossed the Jordan during the conquest of Canaan.

*See* Casluhim, Casluhites.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 10:14; Deuteronomy 2:23; 1 Chronicles 1:12; Jeremiah 47:4; Amos 9:7; Genesis 10:13-14
* **Associated Articles:** Casluhites, Casluhim (ID: `4441@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Crete (ID: `place:Crete`); Caphtorim (ID: `group:Caphtorim`)

## Capital (id: 4420)

CAPITAL
=======

In architecture (building design), the capital is the top part of a pillar. Capitals are often decorated with detailed designs. Capitals (“chapiter” in the King James Version) were on top of the five pillars of the tabernacle during the Israelite's wandering in the wilderness ([Exodus 36:38](https://ref.ly/Exod36:38)). They were also on top of the pillars called “Boaz” and “Jachin” in Solomon's temple ([1 Kings 7:16–22, 40–42](https://ref.ly/1Kgs7:16-1Kgs7:22,1Kgs7:40-1Kgs7:42)).

*See also* Architecture.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 36:38; 1 Kings 7:16-22; 1 Kings 7:40-42
* **Associated Articles:** Architecture (ID: `3720@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Capital (ID: `realia:Capital`)

## Capital Punishment (id: 4419)

CAPITAL PUNISHMENT
==================

The practice of killing someone as a legal penalty for certain crimes.

*See* Criminal Law and Punishment.

* **Associated Articles:** Criminal Law and Punishment (ID: `4693@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Cappadocia (id: 4421)

CAPPADOCIA
==========

Cappadocia was a high plateau region in eastern Asia Minor. Several mountain ranges crossed the region.

The name Cappadocia does not occur in the Hebrew Old Testament. Some passages mention Caphtor or Caphtorites ([Deuteronomy 2:23](https://ref.ly/Deut2:23); [Amos 9:7](https://ref.ly/Amos9:7)). But the Septuagint (the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament) uses the name “Cappadocia.” A few scholars suggest that Cappadocia was the original home of the Philistines.

In the New Testament, some visitors to Jerusalem came from Cappadocia. On the day of Pentecost, they were amazed to hear people speaking in their own languages ([Acts 2:5–13](https://ref.ly/Acts2:5-Acts2:13)). Later, Christians lived in Cappadocia and other parts of Asia Minor. The apostle Peter addressed his first letter to these believers ([1 Peter 1:1](https://ref.ly/1Pet1:1)).

Pontus bordered Cappadocia on the north. Syria and Armenia bordered it on the east. Cilicia bordered Cappadocia on the south. Lycaonia bordered it on the west. 

Cappadocia was famous for its wheat, cattle, and horses. It also exported alabaster, mica, silver, and lead. Important trade routes extended throughout the region. One route went through the Cilician Gates northward to Pontus. One after another, different people groups controlled or dominated the area. First the Hittites, then Assyrians, Babylonians, Persians, Greeks, Seleucids, and Romans.

First Maccabees refers to a letter sent to Ariarathes, the king of Cappadocia ([1 Maccabees 15:22](https://ref.ly/1Macc15:22)). This may show that a large Jewish community lived there by the early second century BC. Some Jews from this community were probably in Jerusalem at the time of Pentecost. Christianity may later have spread north into Cappadocia along the road from Tarsus. By the fourth century AD, Cappadocia had become known for several important Christian church leaders.

* **Associated Passages:** Deuteronomy 2:23; Amos 9:7; 1 Peter 1:1; 1 Maccabees 15:22; Acts 2:5-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cappadocia (ID: `place:Cappadocia`)

## Captivity, the (id: 4422)

CAPTIVITY, The
==============

The period when many people from Judah were taken to Babylonia after Nebuchadnezzar’s conquest of Jerusalem in the sixth century BC. *See* Diaspora of the Jews.

* **Associated Articles:** Diaspora of the Jews (ID: `4831@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Caravan (id: 4423)

CARAVAN
=======

A caravan was a group of merchants, pilgrims, or other travelers who journeyed together in Bible times. Traveling as a group gave them greater protection. They usually used pack animals to carry goods and personal belongings. People mainly used donkeys to transport goods from one region to another until about 1100 BC. After that time, camels became more common.

Ancient Palestine lay between the Mediterranean Sea and Egypt on one side and Syria, Mesopotamia, Arabia, and lands farther east on the other. Many trade routes crossed the region. As a result, the people of Israel knew caravans well. 

In Old Testament times, many caravans came from Transjordan, the region east of the Jordan River, and from Arabia. Arabian caravans often carried spices and incense. These products could bring large profits. The rulers of Sheba took part in this trade ([1 Kings 10:2](https://ref.ly/1Kgs10:2)). 

The size of a caravan depended on the amount of trade, the danger of the route, and the number of camels available. A caravan might include about 40 camels. Ropes could connect the saddle of one camel to the nose ring of the camel behind it. The camels could travel in a single line or in rows of three or four.

In hot weather or on long journey, a camel could carry about 159 kilograms (350 pounds). On short trips with cool weather, a camel could carry much more. 

Joseph's brothers sold him into slavery to a caravan that was carrying spices to Egypt ([Genesis 37:25–28](https://ref.ly/Gen37:25-Gen37:28)). Groups traveling to attack other people also sometimes formed caravans ([Judges 6:3–5](https://ref.ly/Judg6:3-Judg6:5); [1 Samuel 30:1–20](https://ref.ly/1Sam30:1-1Sam30:20)).

*See also* Travel.

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Kings 10:2; 1 Samuel 30:1-20; Genesis 37:25-28; Judges 6:3-5
* **Associated Articles:** Travel (ID: `8915@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

## Carchemish (id: 4427)

CARCHEMISH
==========

Carchemish was an ancient city beside an important crossing point on the west bank of the upper Euphrates River. It was about 104\.6 kilometers (65 miles) northeast of Aleppo. Today, part of the ruins lies in Turkey and part in Syria. The meaning of the name is uncertain. Discoveries at Ebla suggest that it may mean “city of Chemosh,” the name of a Moabite god.

A north–south trade route followed the Euphrates River through Carchemish. An east–west route also passed through the city, linking Nineveh with the Mediterranean Sea.

Pottery found at the site shows that people lived there in prehistoric times. The earliest known reference to Carchemish appears in the Ebla tablets, dating to about 2400 BC. Carchemish lies about is about 120\.7 kilometers (75 miles) west of Haran. Abraham may have passed through the city on his journey to Canaan.

Early in its history, Carchemish was first allied with Mari and later with Aleppo. The Hittites captured the city in 1355 BC. It became the regional capital of eastern Hatti and adopted Hittite culture and language.

The Assyrians tried for several centuries to bring Carchemish into their empire. Sargon II finally conquered the city in 717 BC and made it an Assyrian stronghold in the northwest ([Isaiah 10:9](https://ref.ly/Isa10:9)). 

Archaeological excavations show that Carchemish included both Hittite and Assyrian styles of architecture. The city had a casemate wall, a double wall with spaces between its sections. It stood above sloping earthworks that made attacks more difficult.

A fortified citadel stood at the highest point of the city and had its own wall. The city also had a palace, a temple, and a large ceremonial staircase.

When the Neo\-Babylonian kingdom under Nebuchadnezzar replaced the Assyrian Empire, Carchemish was the last major Assyrian city to fall, in 605 BC. Pharaoh Neco II of Egypt helped defend it ([2 Chronicles 35:20](https://ref.ly/2Chr35:20); [Jeremiah 46:2](https://ref.ly/Jer46:2)). After this, Carchemish became less important.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 35:20; Isaiah 10:9; Jeremiah 46:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Carchemish (ID: `place:Carchemish`)

## Caria (id: 4429)

CARIA
=====

Caria was a region in the southwest part of Asia Minor (modern\-day Turkey).

When Simon was high priest in Jerusalem, Lucius, a Roman leader, sent a letter to King Ptolemy (who ruled from 145 to 116 BC). The letter talked about friendship between Rome and Simon.

Caria was one of several Greek regions that received this letter ([1 Maccabees 15:23](https://ref.ly/1Macc15:23)). In the letter, Lucius said that anyone who ran away from Israel to these regions should be sent back to Simon. Simon would then punish them according to Jewish law (verse [21](https://ref.ly/1Macc15:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Maccabees 15:21; 1 Maccabees 15:23


## Carmel (id: 4430)

CARMEL
======

1. A mountain ridge that stretches about 32\.2 kilometers (20 miles) along the Mediterranean Sea and extends southeast into the Jezreel Valley. Its widest point at the southeast is 20\.9 kilometers (13 miles). Its highest peak stands at 530\.7 meters (1,742 feet). The ridge is made of the same limestone as the central mountains of Palestine.

    The mountain forms a point of land that extends into the sea south of the Bay of Acre. The modern city of Haifa, which is built on different levels of the hillside on the northwest corner of Carmel, has excellent harbor facilities. Several Jewish settlements and two large Druze villages are also located on the slopes of Mount Carmel. (Druzes are members of a distinct sect within Islam.) The plain of Sharon extends to the south.

    Mount Carmel was known for its beauty and fertile land ([Isaiah 33:9](https://ref.ly/Isa33:9); [35:2](https://ref.ly/Isa35:2)). In ancient times, it was covered with oak forests, olive trees, and vineyards. The name “Carmel” comes from a Hebrew word that means “vineyard” or “garden of God.” The mountain was so thick with wild plants that, along with its gorges and caves, it became a hideout for robbers and people rejected by society ([Amos 9:3](https://ref.ly/Amos9:3)).

    Today, Mount Carmel is still forested, and large areas have been turned into a nature reserve. In [Song of Solomon 7:5](https://ref.ly/Song7:5), the poet describes his beloved, saying, "Your head crowns you like Mount Carmel," perhaps suggesting her hair was thick and full like the many trees on the mountain.

    Mount Carmel’s rocky terrain was a barrier to north\-south trade and military routes. Most conquerors and traders went around it. They traveled through the Jezreel Valley to the east or the Zebulun Valley to the northeast. However, important passes cut through the mountain. One narrow pass at the southern end connects the plains of Sharon and Esdraelon. Pharaoh Thutmose III used this pass in the 15th century BC. British General Lord Allenby used it in 1918 when he conquered Palestine. The tribal lands of Asher, Zebulun, Issachar, and Manasseh met at Mount Carmel, but it seems that none of the tribes fully controlled the mountain’s heights.

    Mount Carmel also held religious importance. It was the site of the famous confrontation between the prophet Elijah and the prophets of Baal ([1 Kings 18](https://ref.ly/1Kgs18:1-1Kgs18:46)). This was a good location because Mount Carmel lay between Israel and Phoenicia. It demonstrated the struggle between the Phoenician god Baal and the God of Israel. Elijah was not the first to build an altar to God on the mountain. Before offering his sacrifice, he repaired an old, ruined altar of the Lord ([1 Kings 18:30](https://ref.ly/1Kgs18:30)).

    The event's traditional site is Qeren ha\-Carmel. It is 481\.7 meters (1,581 feet) high and overlooks the Jezreel Valley. The small stream Kishon, mentioned in [1 Kings 18:40](https://ref.ly/1Kgs18:40)) runs through the Jezreel Valley and around the north side of Mount Carmel before emptying into the Bay of Acre.

2. A town in Judah ([Joshua 15:55](https://ref.ly/Josh15:55)) identified with el\-Kirmil (Kermel), 11\.3 kilometers (seven miles) south of Hebron. King Saul set up a memorial to his conquest of the Amalekites there ([1 Samuel 15:12](https://ref.ly/1Sam15:12)).

    Carmel was also the home of Nabal, a grumpy man who refused kindness to David ([1 Samuel 25:2–14](https://ref.ly/1Sam25:2-1Sam25:14)). After Nabal’s death, his beautiful wife, Abigail, married David. Carmel is mentioned as the home of Hezro, one of David’s 30 heroes ([2 Samuel 23:35](https://ref.ly/2Sam23:35)).

* **Associated Passages:** Joshua 15:55; 1 Samuel 15:12; 2 Samuel 23:35; 1 Kings 18:30; 1 Kings 18:40; Song of Songs 7:5; Isaiah 33:9; Isaiah 35:2; Amos 9:3; 1 Kings 18:1-46; 1 Samuel 25:2-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Carmel (ID: `place:Carmel`); Mount Carmel (ID: `place:CarmelMount`)

## Carmi (id: 4432)

CARMI
=====

1. One of Reuben’s sons; he joined his grandfather Jacob in moving to Egypt ([Genesis 46:9](https://ref.ly/Gen46:9); [Exodus 6:14](https://ref.ly/Exod6:14); [1 Chronicles 5:3](https://ref.ly/1Chr5:3)). He founded the family of Carmites ([Numbers 26:5–7](https://ref.ly/Num26:5-Num26:7)).
2. Achan’s father and a member of the tribe of Judah ([Joshua 7:1, 18](https://ref.ly/Josh7:1,Josh7:18); [1 Chronicles 2:7](https://ref.ly/1Chr2:7); [4:1](https://ref.ly/1Chr4:1)).

## Carnaim (id: 4433)

CARNAIM
=======

Carnaim was a strong city in the region of Gilead. It became important after the Babylonian exile. Judas Maccabeus attacked and destroyed Carnaim ([1 Maccabees 5:26, 43–44](https://ref.ly/1Macc5:26,1Macc5:43-1Macc5:44); [2 Maccabees 12:21–23, 26](https://ref.ly/2Macc12:21-2Macc12:23,2Macc12:26)). He also destroyed the temple in the city. This temple was for Atargatis, a Syrian goddess linked to fish.

*See* Ashteroth\-karnaim.

## Carnelian (id: 4434)

CARNELIAN
=========

Carnelian is a type of chalcedony, which is a form of quartz. Its color can range from deep red to pale pink or almost white. It is made of silicon and oxygen. Some Bible translations use “carnelian” and “sardius” for the same stone. Sardius is a type of deep brown or red quartz

The Bible lists carnelian as one of the foundation jewels in the wall of the new Jerusalem ([Revelation 21:20](https://ref.ly/Rev21:20)). 

*See also* Precious Stones.

* **Associated Passages:** Revelation 21:20
* **Associated Articles:** Precious Stones (ID: `8628@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Carob Tree (id: 4435)

CAROB TREE
==========

An evergreen tree that belongs to the bean family and is common throughout the Middle East. It produces edible seeds contained in a pod. The seeds look similar to peas.

Scholars generally agree that the "husks" mentioned in Jesus's parable of the prodigal son were the pods of the carob or locust tree ([Luke 15:16](https://ref.ly/Luke15:16)). The carob (*Ceratonia siliqua*) is an attractive evergreen tree that grows commonly throughout Israel and the surrounding areas, Syria, and Egypt. The pods are most plentiful in April and May. They contain numerous seeds surrounded by a sweet, pleasant\-tasting pulp.

These pods were widely used in ancient times, as they are today. They were used as food for cattle, horses, and pigs. During times of food shortage, people also ate the pods. It is possible those who were very poor ate them regularly. The Talmud frequently mentions the carob as a good food source for domestic animals.

The seeds of the carob were once used as a standard of weight and are the origin of the term "carat." Some commentators suggest that the "locusts" that John the Baptist ate were not insects but actually the fruit of the carob tree ([Matthew 3:4](https://ref.ly/Matt3:4)).

* **Associated Articles:** Plants (ID: `7761@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Carpenter (id: 4436)

CARPENTER
=========

One who worked with wood, building the framework of houses, the roofing, windows, and doors. Often small structures like houses were built by the owner. Temples and palaces required skilled workers. For these larger structures, carpenters worked alongside stonemasons, skilled workers who knew how to cut and prepare stone for building ([2 Kings 12:11](https://ref.ly/2Kgs12:11); [22:6](https://ref.ly/2Kgs22:6); [1 Chronicles 14:1](https://ref.ly/1Chr14:1); [22:15](https://ref.ly/1Chr22:15); [2 Chronicles 24:12](https://ref.ly/2Chr24:12); [34:11](https://ref.ly/2Chr34:11); [Ezra 3:7](https://ref.ly/Ezra3:7)). Carpentry is rarely referred to in the New Testament, though it was the profession of Jesus and his father Joseph ([Matthew 13:55](https://ref.ly/Matt13:55); [Mark 6:3](https://ref.ly/Mark6:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Kings 12:11; 2 Kings 22:6; 1 Chronicles 14:1; 1 Chronicles 22:15; 2 Chronicles 24:12; 2 Chronicles 34:11; Ezra 3:7; Matthew 13:55; Mark 6:3


## Carpus (id: 4437)

CARPUS
======

A man with whom the apostle Paul left his cloak at the city of Troas in Asia Minor. Paul instructed his disciple Timothy to bring the cloak when he visited him in prison ([2 Timothy 4:13](https://ref.ly/2Tim4:13)). It is possible that Carpus was one of the converts of Paul. According to tradition, Carpus became the bishop of Berytus (Beirut) in Thrace. 

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Timothy 4:13

===============

## Casluhites, Casluhim (id: 4441)

CASLUHITES, CASLUHIM
====================

The Casluthites (called Casluhim in some Bible translations) were descendants of Noah through his son Ham and his grandson Mizraim, who is called Egypt in some translations. They were ancestors of the Philistines ([Genesis 10:14](https://ref.ly/Gen10:14); [1 Chronicles 1:12](https://ref.ly/1Chr1:12)).

## Caspin (id: 4442)

CASPIN
======

Caspin was a town east of the Jordan River. It was probably the same place as Chaspho ([1 Maccabees 5:26, 36](https://ref.ly/1Macc5:26,1Macc5:36)). Ancient and modern experts do not fully agree on the location. The Jewish historian Josephus identifies Caspin with Maked ([2 Maccabees 12:13](https://ref.ly/2Macc12:13)). In [1 Maccabees 5:24–36](https://ref.ly/1Macc5:24-1Macc5:36), Judas and Jonathan Maccabeus led their army into the region called Transjordan (across the Jordan River). There Judas “took Chaspho, Maked, and Bosor, and the other cities of Gilead” (verse[36](https://ref.ly/1Macc5:36), Revised Standard Version).

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Maccabees 5:26; 1 Maccabees 5:36; 2 Maccabees 12:13; 1 Maccabees 5:24-36


## Cassia (id: 4443)

CASSIA
======

A type of tree that grows in tropical Asia. The bark of cassia is used as a spice that tastes similar to cinnamon but is not as high quality. 

The "cassia" mentioned in the Bible comes from the cassia bark tree (*Cinnamomum cassia*). It is one of the ingredients used in holy anointing oil ([Exodus 30:24](https://ref.ly/Exod30:24)). It was also a valuable trade item ([Ezekiel 27:19](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:19)). comes from the cassia bark tree (*Cinnamomum cassia*). 

In [Psalm 45:8](https://ref.ly/Ps45:8), the reference seems to be to a different plant called Indian orris (*Saussurea lappa*).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 30:24; Ezekiel 27:19
* **Associated Articles:** Plants (ID: `7761@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cassia (ID: `flora:Cassia`)

## Castanet (id: 4444)

CASTANET
========

A small hand\-held percussion instrument, like a rattle . These are called "sistrums" in [2 Samuel 6:5](https://ref.ly/2Sam6:5). 

*See* Musical Instruments; Music.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Samuel 6:5

## Castor and Pollux (id: 4445)

CASTOR AND POLLUX
=================

According to Greek and Roman mythology, these were twin sons of Zeus. The apostle Paul sailed from Malta to Puteoli on a ship whose sign or figurehead was the "Twin Brothers" ([Acts 28:11](https://ref.ly/Acts28:11)). These twin brothers were also known as the Dioscuri (which means "sons of Zeus").

*See* Dioscuri.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:11
* **Associated Articles:** Dioscuri (ID: `4854@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Castor Oil Plant (id: 716003)

Castor Oil Plant
================

A large plant native to tropical Africa and Asia. The castor oil plant (*Ricinus communis*)is grown both for its appearance and for the oil that comes from its seeds. The gourd mentioned in [Jonah 4:6–7](https://ref.ly/Jonah4:6-Jonah4:7) was probably this plant.

The castor bean is a soft\-stemmed shrub that grows 0\.9 to 3\.7 meters (3 to 12 feet tall). It has very large leaves that look like an open human hand. The plant can be found growing in waste places, especially near water. People grow the castor oil plant in both Lebanon and Israel and the surrounding areas. In hot climates, it can grow as tall as a tree and provides good shade because of its many large, umbrella\-like leaves. In the countries of Asia, it is known for how quickly it grows.

The oil taken from the castor bean seeds was used by the Jewish people in religious ceremonies. Rabbinical tradition approved it as one of five kinds of oil allowed for such use. It is important to note that while the oil is useful, the seeds themselves are poisonous if eaten.



## Caterpillar (id: 4446)

CATERPILLAR
===========

Wormlike larva of a butterfly, moth, and some other insects. A larva is the young form of some insects that goes through a complete change before becoming an adult. Such insects pass through four stages:

1. Egg
2. Larva
3. Pupa
4. Adult

The caterpillar is the larval stage of insects. Bees, flies, moths, and butterflies all have a larval or caterpillar stage.

The word “caterpillar” occurs three times in certain translations, like the New Living Translation in [1 Kings 8:37](https://ref.ly/1Kgs8:37), [2 Chronicles 6:28](https://ref.ly/2Chr6:28), and [Psalm 78:46](https://ref.ly/Ps78:46). In the book of Joel, the same word is translated “locust” ([Joel 1:4](https://ref.ly/Joel1:4); [2:25](https://ref.ly/Joel2:25)). The locust and grasshopper do not have a full transformation. They have only three stages: 

1. Egg
2. Nymph
3. Adult

The nymph is a tiny adult. Its wings are not fully developed, though their outline may be present. There are several nymph stages known as instars. The reference to locust refers to the last instars. The wing structures are still folded and enclosed in a sac. But, they are recognizable. That form of the insect is about two and a half centimeters (one inch) long.

*See* Locust.

* **Associated Articles:** Animals (ID: `3614@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Locusts (ID: `6766@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Catholic Letters (id: 4447)

CATHOLIC LETTERS
================

A traditional name for seven New Testament letters is the Catholic Letters. These letters are: 

* James;
* 1 and 2 Peter;
* 1, 2, and 3 John; and
* Jude.

The word “catholic” means “general” or “universal.” Scholars have understood this name in several ways: 

1. These letters express the views of all the apostles.
2. They are canonical or genuine.
3. Thus they were distinguished from works containing false beliefs written around the same time.
4. The most common meaning is that these letters were written for a wide group of believers. They were not written mainly to one local church, as some of the apostle Paul’s letters were. However, 2 John and 3 John are exceptions. Second John is written to a woman or to a local church. Third John is written to one person.

*See also* James, Letter of; John, Letters of; Jude, Letter of; Peter, First Letter of; Peter, Second Letter of.

* **Associated Articles:** James, Letter of (ID: `6201@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Letters of John (ID: `6410@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Jude, Letter of (ID: `6488@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Peter, First Letter of (ID: `7678@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Peter, Second Letter of (ID: `7679@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Cattle (id: 4448)

CATTLE
======

Cattle are large farm animals that people raise for meat, milk, and other uses. Cattle typically refers to cows and oxen, but in the Bible "cattle" sometimes also refers to sheep and goats. 

Early Uses of Cattle
--------------------

The Old Testament often emphasized the beauty of cattle. Egypt, especially the area called Goshen near the Nile River, had many cattle. The Hebrew people lived there when Joseph brought them to Egypt.

Early peoples may have first kept cattle mainly for milk rather than meat. They got their meat from wild animals they hunted. Cattle provided strong hides for shields, replacing wood. They used their dung as fuel when there was not enough wood ([Ezra 4:15](https://ref.ly/Ezek4:15)). Cattle were also used for heavy loads and plowing. Farmers used cattle to pull wheeled transport more than any other animal.

Types of Cattle
---------------

The Bible uses “cattle” to refer to all trained animals or livestock ([Genesis 1:24](https://ref.ly/Gen1:24); [2:20](https://ref.ly/Gen2:20); [7:23](https://ref.ly/Gen7:23); [47:6, 16–17](https://ref.ly/Gen47:6,Gen47:16-Gen47:17); [Exodus 9:3–7](https://ref.ly/Exod9:3-Exod9:7); [Numbers 3:41, 45](https://ref.ly/Num3:41,Num3:45)). Sometimes, it meant all large trained animals ([Numbers 31:9](https://ref.ly/Num31:9); [32:26](https://ref.ly/Num32:26)). However, sometimes the King James Version uses cattle to refer only to sheep and goats ([Genesis 30:32, 39, 43](https://ref.ly/Gen30:32,Gen30:39,Gen30:43); [31:8, 10](https://ref.ly/Gen31:8,Gen31:10); [Isaiah 7:25](https://ref.ly/Isa7:25); [43:23](https://ref.ly/Isa43:23)).

Various cattle types were tamed in the holy land. In southern Judah, small, short\-legged, black or brown shorthorn cattle thrived. They were easy to train and vital for farming. Along the coast, a larger cattle type was found. Meanwhile, the wild areas east of the Jordan River were home to large black cattle.

Breeding and Laws about Cattle
------------------------------

Cattle breeding was widely practiced by the patriarchs (see [Genesis 32:15](https://ref.ly/Gen32:15); [Job 21:10](https://ref.ly/Job21:10)). Strict laws in Mesopotamia, as well as in Israel, penalized the owner of a bull that gored a man or other cattle ([Exodus 21:28–36](https://ref.ly/Exod21:28-Exod21:36)). Bulls were sometimes used as a symbol of strength or violence ([Deuteronomy 33:17](https://ref.ly/Deut33:17); [Psalms 22:12](https://ref.ly/Ps22:12); [68:30](https://ref.ly/Ps68:30); [Isaiah 10:13](https://ref.ly/Isa10:13)). Usually, one bull can breed with about 30 cows. However, more bulls were kept in Israel. This was because bulls were often used for general sacrifices ([Leviticus 22:23](https://ref.ly/Lev22:23); [Numbers 23:1](https://ref.ly/Num23:1)) or for special sacrifices ([Judges 6:25](https://ref.ly/Judg6:25); [1 Samuel 1:24](https://ref.ly/1Sam1:24)).

Particular sacrifices were offered at the: 

* anointing of priests ([Exodus 29:1](https://ref.ly/Exod29:1))
* Blessing of an altar ([Numbers 7](https://ref.ly/Num7:1-Num7:89))
* Purification of the Levites ([Numbers 8](https://ref.ly/Num8:1-Num8:26))
* Sin offerings ([Leviticus 16](https://ref.ly/Lev16:1-Lev16:34))
* Day of the new moon ([Numbers 28:11–14](https://ref.ly/Num28:11-Num28:14))
* Passover ([Numbers 28:19](https://ref.ly/Num28:19))
* Feast of Weeks ([Numbers 28:27](https://ref.ly/Num28:27))
* Feast of Trumpets ([Numbers 29:1–2](https://ref.ly/Num29:1-Num29:2))
* Day of Atonement ([Numbers 29:7–9](https://ref.ly/Num29:7-Num29:9))
* Feast of Tabernacles ([Numbers 29:12–38](https://ref.ly/Num29:12-Num29:38))

The Feast of Tabernacles required the most bulls for burnt offerings of all the annual feasts. A total of 71 were slaughtered over eight days.

Calves in the Bible
-------------------

Calves were sometimes called “sons of the herd” in the original Hebrew ([Genesis 18:8](https://ref.ly/Gen18:8); [1 Samuel 6:7](https://ref.ly/1Sam6:7); [14:32](https://ref.ly/1Sam14:32)). The calf was a symbol of peacefulness ([Isaiah 11:6](https://ref.ly/Isa11:6)). It was also used to refer to the weak ([Psalm 68:30](https://ref.ly/Ps68:30)). A calf’s head decorated the back of Solomon’s throne ([1 Kings 10:19](https://ref.ly/1Kgs10:19)). Calves were sometimes fed in stalls to keep them from running and losing weight in the field ([Amos 6:4](https://ref.ly/Amos6:4); [Malachi 4:2](https://ref.ly/Mal4:2); [Luke 15:23](https://ref.ly/Luke15:23)). They may also have been kept around the house. The witch of Endor kept a calf in her house. She killed it and served it to Saul and his men ([1 Samuel 28:24–25](https://ref.ly/1Sam28:24-1Sam28:25)). Calves supplied veal ([Genesis 18:7](https://ref.ly/Gen18:7)), a delicacy of the rich. Amos criticized the wealthy for their luxurious, careless lives. He mentioned the stall\-fattened calves ([Amos 6:4](https://ref.ly/Amos6:4)).Calves also supplied meat for all Saul’s armies at the great slaughter of the Philistines ([1 Samuel 14:32](https://ref.ly/1Sam14:32)). The “fatted calf” served roasted or boiled was gourmet fare, suitable for the finest banquet ([Genesis 18:7](https://ref.ly/Gen18:7); [Matthew 22:4](https://ref.ly/Matt22:4); [Luke 15:23](https://ref.ly/Luke15:23)).

Cattle were included in the law of firstborn ([Exodus 13:12](https://ref.ly/Exod13:12)). They were a sign of wealth ([Genesis 13:2](https://ref.ly/Gen13:2)) and were allowed to be spoils of war ([Joshua 8:2](https://ref.ly/Josh8:2)). Aaron, the first high priest, made a golden calf to rival the ark of the covenant ([Exodus 32](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1-Exod32:35); [Deuteronomy 9:16, 21](https://ref.ly/Deut9:16,Deut9:21)). hough he called the calf an image of the invisible God, it was offensive. The calf was a fertility symbol linked to Egyptian and Canaanite practices. Two calves were later made by Jeroboam I of Israel for the shrines at Bethel and Dan ([1 Kings 12:28–33](https://ref.ly/1Kgs12:28-1Kgs12:33)). Hosea’s prophetic rejection of calf worship were directed at those shrines ([Hosea 8:5–6](https://ref.ly/Hos8:5-Hos8:6); [13:2](https://ref.ly/Hos13:2)).

Oxen and Their Uses
-------------------

An ox is an adult bull that has been neutered (had its reproductive organs removed). A young ox is called a steer. People used oxen to do work ([Numbers 7:3](https://ref.ly/Num7:3); [Deuteronomy 22:10](https://ref.ly/Deut22:10); [25:4](https://ref.ly/Deut25:4)). However, cows were used to move heavy objects because they were gentler. 

Oxen were also used as pack animals ([1 Chronicles 12:40](https://ref.ly/1Chr12:40)). However, they had less energy than donkeys, camels, or mules. They usually ate grass ([Numbers 22:4](https://ref.ly/Num22:4); [Psalm 106:20](https://ref.ly/Ps106:20)), but they also ate straw ([Isaiah 11:7](https://ref.ly/Isa11:7)) and salted fodder (food prepared for cattle) ([Isaiah 30:24](https://ref.ly/Isa30:24)). They were kept in stables ([Luke 13:15](https://ref.ly/Luke13:15)). Oxen could not be offered as sacrifices because they had been castrated ([Leviticus 22:24](https://ref.ly/Lev22:24)). They could be used for food but were rarely eaten. In ancient Palestine, an ox and an ass were the bare minimum for survival in the agricultural economy ([Job 24:3](https://ref.ly/Job24:3); compare [Exodus 20:17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:17)).

*See also* Agriculture; Food and Food Preparation; Offerings and Sacrifices.

* **Associated Articles:** Agriculture (ID: `3424@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Animals (ID: `3614@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Offerings and Sacrifices (ID: `7462@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Food and Food Preparation (ID: `5286@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Cauda (id: 4449)

CAUDA
=====

Cauda was a small island south of Crete. The ship carrying the apostle Paul to Rome stopped at Cauda during a storm to find temporary protection ([Acts 27:16](https://ref.ly/Acts27:16)). 

Behind the island, the water was calmer. In this protected area, the sailors pulled on board a small boat they had been towing behind the ship. They also worked to strengthen the ship's hull.

Even after they lowered the sails, the strong winds pushed them past the island. Eventually, the ship was wrecked.

Cauda is the modern island of Gaudos (also called Gozzo). Ancient handwritten copies of the Bible spell the name in different ways. Some use "Cauda" (as in most modern Bible versions), while others use "Clauda" (as in the King James Version and New American Standard Bible).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 27:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cauda (ID: `place:Cauda`)

## Caulk, Caulker, Shipwright (id: 4450)

CAULK, CAULKER, SHIPWRIGHT
==========================

Caulk is a waterproof material, such as bitumen, that is used to seal the seams between the planks of a ship. A caulker (or shipwright) is the person who applies it to make the ship watertight ([Ezekiel 27:9](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:9)).

*See also* Travel.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:9

CAVALRY
=======

Soldiers who fight while riding on horses.

*See* Warfare.

## Cedar (id: 4452)

CEDAR
=====

A tree that grows naturally in Palestine. Its wood was used for building and construction ([1 Kings 6:9](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:9)). Cedar refers to several types of evergreen trees with cones that are native to Europe, Asia, and Africa. Most mentions of "cedar" in the Bible refer to the cedar of Lebanon.

The cedar of Lebanon (*Cedrus libani*) is a magnificent tree. It was the tallest and most impressive tree known to the Israelite people. It grows quite quickly, reaching heights of up to 36\.6 meters (120 feet). Its trunk grows as wide as 2\.4 meters (8 feet) across. During King Solomon's time, these trees were plentiful on the mountains of Lebanon. Today, they are very rare because too many have been cut down for lumber.

People valued the cedar of Lebanon for many reasons: its strength, beauty, long life, pleasant smell, and wood that lasts a very long time without rotting. The tree became a symbol of greatness, power, majesty, dignity, tall stature, and wide spreading branches.

In [Ezekiel 17:3, 22–24](https://ref.ly/Ezek17:3,Ezek17:22-Ezek17:24), and [31:3–18](https://ref.ly/Ezek31:3-Ezek31:18), these towering trees are like kings of the forest. Cedars are used as symbols to represent worldly strength, power, and glory.

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Kings 6:9
* **Associated Articles:** Plants (ID: `7761@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`)

CEDRON
======

## Cenchrea, Cenchreae (id: 4454)

CENCHREA, CENCHREAE
===================

Cenchrea was a seaport about 12\.9 kilometers (eight miles) east of Corinth. It served the shipping needs of that larger city.

Historical records mention Cenchrea as early as the fifth century BC, when Athens attacked Corinth. Before the Corinthian Canal was built across the narrow strip of land, goods and travelers often passed from Cenchrea through Corinth to Lechaion on their way from Asia to Europe.

Excavations began in 1963\. Archaeologists found the harbor breakwater, the remains of warehouses from the early first century, and a large stone building from the second century.

A church from the fourth century shows that Christianity had become influential in the city. Parts of the ancient road from the Cenchrean Gate in Corinth can still be seen among the ruins of the city’s agora, or marketplace.

The New Testament mentions Cenchrea twice. The apostle Paul took an oath requiring the cutting of his hair before leaving Cenchrea during his third missionary journey ([Acts 18:18](https://ref.ly/Acts18:18)). In his letter to the church in Rome, Paul commended Phoebe, a deaconess of the church in Cenchrea. Phoebe was well\-known for her Christian service ([Romans 16:1](https://ref.ly/Rom16:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 18:18; Romans 16:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cenchreae (ID: `place:Cenchreae`)

## Cendebeus (id: 4455)

CENDEBEUS
=========

Cendebeus was the commander\-in\-chief of the seacoast of Syria\-Palestine about 138 BC under Antiochus VII ([1 Maccabees 15:38](https://ref.ly/1Macc15:38)). Cendebeus was ordered to build a fortress at Kedron (which may be the Gederah from [Joshua 15:36](https://ref.ly/Josh15:36)). After that, he moved his base to Jamnia and raided Judea ([1 Maccabees 15:39–40](https://ref.ly/1Macc15:39-1Macc15:40)). 

Simon Maccabeus was too old to fight, so he sent his sons, Judas and John Hyrcanus, against Cendebeus. He gave them 20,000 soldiers and horsemen. Judas was wounded, but the Jews prevailed after slaying about 2,000 men. Cendebeus was pursued back to Kedron, and the Jews returned to Judea ([1 Maccabees 16:1–10](https://ref.ly/1Macc16:1-1Macc16:10)).

* **Associated Passages:** Joshua 15:36; 1 Maccabees 15:38; 1 Maccabees 15:39-40; 1 Maccabees 16:1-10


## Censer (id: 4456)

CENSER
======

A censer is a container used to burn incense. On the Day of Atonement, the high priest burned two handfuls of incense in the censer. He did this inside the most holy place before the Lord ([Leviticus 16:12](https://ref.ly/Lev16:12)). The censers of the tabernacle were bronze ([Numbers 16:39](https://ref.ly/Num16:39)). The censers used by the angels in the book of Revelation were gold ([Revelation 8:3–5](https://ref.ly/Rev8:3-Rev8:5)).

*See also* Tabernacle; Temple.

* **Associated Passages:** Leviticus 16:12; Numbers 16:39; Revelation 8:3-5
* **Associated Articles:** Tabernacle (ID: `8696@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Temple (ID: `8782@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Censer (ID: `realia:Censer`)

## Census (id: 4457)

CENSUS
======

The registration and counting of people, usually for war or taxes. The Bible mentions a few censuses.

The first census was taken at Mount Sinai two years after the exodus. It counted Israelite males over 20 years old to assess military strength—603,550 in total ([Numbers 1:1–3, 46](https://ref.ly/Num1:1-Num1:3)). A special census of Levites, who served in the tabernacle instead of military duties, counted 22,000 males, with only 8,580 eligible for priestly service ([Numbers 3:15, 39](https://ref.ly/Num3:15,Num3:39); [4:46](https://ref.ly/Num4:46-Num4:48)[–](https://ref.ly/Num4:46-Num4:48)[48](https://ref.ly/Num4:46-Num4:48)).

The second census occurred at the end of Israel’s 40 years in the wilderness. This is recorded in [Numbers 26](https://ref.ly/Num26:1-Num26:65). It was also a military census, taken just before the Israelites invaded the Promised Land. The census found 601,730 men able to fight ([Numbers 26:51](https://ref.ly/Num26:51)), not including the Levites. The 23,000 Levites were counted separately because they would not receive land ([Numbers 26:62](https://ref.ly/Num26:62)). The Israelites each paid half a shekel, about one\-fifth of an ounce (6 grams) of silver, as part of this census ([Exodus 30:11–16](https://ref.ly/Exod30:11-Exod30:16)).

The third census happened near the end of King David’s reign ([2 Samuel 24:1–17](https://ref.ly/2Sam24:1-2Sam24:17)). God commanded the first two censuses, but David’s census took place when God was angry with Israel. The Bible says the Lord “incited David against them,” but it does not explain David’s reasons (see [1 Chronicles 21:1](https://ref.ly/1Chr21:1) for a later interpretation). David might have wanted the census for conscription, taxation, or to measure his power. Joab, David’s top military commander, felt that taking the census was wrong and tried to stop David. After the census—though there is some uncertainty about whether it was completed (see [1 Chronicles 21:6](https://ref.ly/1Chr21:6); [27:23–24](https://ref.ly/1Chr27:23-1Chr27:24))—David realized his mistake and repented. But God was still angry and gave David a choice of three punishments: three years of famine, three months of fleeing from an enemy, or three days of a deadly plague. David chose the plague, which killed 70,000 men. The census found 800,000 able\-bodied men in Israel and 500,000 in Judah ([2 Samuel 24:9](https://ref.ly/2Sam24:9)). A different account mentions a potential militia of 1,100,000 in Israel and 470,000 in Judah ([1 Chronicles 21:5](https://ref.ly/1Chr21:5)), plus 38,000 Levites who could serve in the temple ([1 Chronicles 23:3](https://ref.ly/1Chr23:3)).

Scholars have questioned why the numbers from the third census are almost twice as large as those from the first two. Many explanations have been suggested, but none are fully satisfying.

A fourth census is recorded in [Ezra 2](https://ref.ly/Ezra2:1-Ezra2:70), which took place when the exiles returned to Jerusalem. It included 42,360 Israelite males, 7,337 slaves (both male and female), and 200 singers (both male and female).

In the New Testament, a census played a role in the events of Jesus’s birth. “Now in those days a decree went out from Caesar Augustus that a census should be taken of the whole empire. This was the first census to take place while Quirinius was governor of Syria. And everyone went to his own town to register" ([Luke 2:1–3](https://ref.ly/Luke2:1-Luke2:3)).

Josephus, a first\-century AD Jewish historian, notes that Quirinius completed a census soon after becoming governor of Syria in AD 6\. But [Matthew 2](https://ref.ly/Matt2:1-Matt2:23) places Jesus’s birth during the reign of Herod the Great, who died in 4 BC, suggesting there were probably two different censuses around that time. Luke’s reference to the “first enrollment” ([Luke 2:2](https://ref.ly/Luke2:2)) likely distinguishes it from the census of AD 6–7\. Luke probably knew about the later census, which he mentions in [Acts 5:37](https://ref.ly/Acts5:37). A series of censuses in Egypt around the same time supports the idea that a similar series occurred in Palestine. The most likely explanation is that an earlier census took place during Quirinius's leadership before he officially became governor.

Luke’s mention of the census under Quirinius serves two purposes. It dates Jesus’s birth and explains why Joseph and Mary were in Bethlehem. The census was likely for tax purposes since the Romans did not require Jews to serve in the military. The requirement to return to one’s hometown reflects both Hebrew traditions and the Roman emperor Caesar Augustus’s willingness to let the Jews follow their customs.

* **Associated Passages:** Numbers 1:46; Numbers 3:15; Numbers 3:39; Numbers 26:51; Numbers 26:62; 2 Samuel 24:9; 1 Chronicles 21:1; 1 Chronicles 21:5; 1 Chronicles 21:6; 1 Chronicles 23:3; Luke 2:2; Acts 5:37; Numbers 1:1-3; Numbers 4:46-48; Numbers 26:1-65; Exodus 30:11-16; 2 Samuel 24:1-17; 1 Chronicles 27:23-24; Ezra 2:1-70; Luke 2:1-3; Matthew 2:1-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Census (ID: `keyterm:Census`)

## Centurion (id: 4458)

CENTURION
=========

A centurion was a leader in the Roman army who commanded 100 soldiers. The Roman army organized its soldiers into groups. Each large unit (called a legion) had 10 groups of 1,000 soldiers. Each group had six centurions who reported to senior officers called tribunes. For example, in [Acts 22:26](https://ref.ly/Acts22:26), a centurion asked his officer what to do about Paul. Centurions had a lot of power because they worked directly with the soldiers. They went to battles with them and made quick choices based on what was happening. 

Being a centurion was often the highest position a normal soldier could reach. Many centurions started as regular soldiers and moved up because of their experience. After becoming a centurion, they could get even better jobs by moving to more important positions. The best job was being the main centurion of the first group of 1000 soldiers in a legion. This meant centurions often moved around the Roman Empire.

Centurions had many jobs besides keeping soldiers in line. They were in charge of death sentences ([Matthew 27:54](https://ref.ly/Matt27:54); [Mark 15:39, 44–45](https://ref.ly/Mark15:39,Mark15:44-Mark15:45); [Luke 23:47](https://ref.ly/Luke23:47)). They were in charge of their soldiers all the time, whether the soldiers were Romans or hired from other places. Being a centurion was respected and paid well. Most people who became centurions stayed in that job for a long time.

The New Testament talks about six centurions. At least two of them seem to have become followers of Christ.

1. A centurion at Capernaum asked Jesus to save his dying servant. He believed that illnesses would listen to Jesus just like his soldiers listened to him ([Matthew 8:5–13](https://ref.ly/Matt8:5-Matt8:13); [Luke 7:2–10](https://ref.ly/Luke7:2-Luke7:10)). Even though he was important, he was humble and knew he needed help. He cared for his servant. Jesus was amazed by his faith and healed the sick man.
2. The centurion in charge of the group that killed Jesus said, “Truly this man was the Son of God!” ([Mark 15:39](https://ref.ly/Mark15:39)) and “Surely this was a righteous man.” ([Luke 23:47](https://ref.ly/Luke23:47)). The apocryphal Acts of Pilate, likely from the fourth century, called this centurion Longinus. Roman Catholics think of him as a saint. There is a stone statue of him in Saint Peter's Basilica in Rome by the 17th\-century artist Giovanni Bernini.
3. A centurion in Caesarea named Cornelius became a follower of Jesus after Peter told him about the gospel. Peter did not want to share the gospel with non\-Jews at first, but God showed him a vision that changed his mind ([Acts 10](https://ref.ly/Acts10:1-Acts10:48)).
4. A centurion in [Acts 22:25–26](https://ref.ly/Acts22:25-Acts22:26) helped save Paul from being beaten when he told his higher officer that Paul was a Roman citizen.
5. Another centurion helped save Paul from Jews who wanted to kill him ([Acts 23:17–22](https://ref.ly/Acts23:17-Acts23:22)).
6. A centurion named Julius had to guard Paul on his trip from Caesarea to Rome ([Acts 27:1](https://ref.ly/Acts27:1)). When their ship broke in a storm, Julius stopped the soldiers from killing all the prisoners, including Paul ([Acts 27:42–43](https://ref.ly/Acts27:42-Acts27:43)).

*See also* Warfare.

* **Associated Passages:** Matthew 27:54; Mark 15:39; Luke 23:47; Acts 22:26; Acts 27:1; Acts 10:1-48; Mark 15:44-45; Matthew 8:5-13; Luke 7:2-10; Acts 22:25-26; Acts 23:17-22; Acts 27:42-43
* **Associated Articles:** Warfare (ID: `9029@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** A Centurion (ID: `person:Centurion`)

## Cephas (id: 4459)

CEPHAS
======

The Aramaic translation for the name of the apostle Simon Peter in [John 1:42](https://ref.ly/John1:42); [1 Corinthians 1:12](https://ref.ly/1Cor1:12); and [Galatians 1:18](https://ref.ly/Gal1:18). The name means "rock" or "stone." *See* Simon Peter.

* **Associated Passages:** John 1:42; 1 Corinthians 1:12; Galatians 1:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Peter (ID: `person:Peter`)

## Cereal Offering (id: 4460)

CEREAL OFFERING
===============

Cereal offering is the term used in the Revised Standard Version (RSV) for what is often called a grain offering. It was one of the offerings given to God in the Old Testament. The cereal offering showed a person's dedication and commitment to God.

*See* Offerings and Sacrifices.

* **Associated Articles:** Offerings and Sacrifices (ID: `7462@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Cerinthus (id: 4461)

CERINTHUS
=========

Cerinthus was a false teacher who lived around AD 100\. The apostle John opposed his teachings.

What Did Cerinthus Believe?
---------------------------

Cerinthus was probably born in Egypt and raised as a Jew. Later, he became the leader of a group with Gnostic beliefs. Gnostics believed that special knowledge could save people. Cerinthus taught that:

* God did not create the world. Instead, a lesser spirit (called the “Demiurge”) or group of angels did.
* One of these angels gave the law to the Jews.
* Jesus was just an ordinary man.
* “The Christ” (a divine power) came down on Jesus at his baptism.
* This “Christ” showed people the true God, who was far above all other beings.
* Before Jesus was crucified, the ”Christ” left him.

John Opposes Cerinthus
----------------------

A well\-known Christian named Irenaeus (who lived around 260–340 AD) told a story about Cerinthus. He said that a student of the apostle John, named Polycarp, shared this story with him.

According to the story, John once went to a public bathhouse in the city of Ephesus. But when John heard that Cerinthus was inside, he ran out. He said, “The building will collapse because the enemy of truth is inside!”

Some people believe that parts of John’s writings in the Bible were written to correct false teachings like those of Cerinthus (see [John 1:1–3, 14](https://ref.ly/John1:1-John1:3,John1:14); [1 John 4:1–3](https://ref.ly/1John4:1-1John4:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** John 1:14; John 1:1-3; 1 John 4:1-3


## Certificate of Divorce (id: 4462)

CERTIFICATE OF DIVORCE
======================

An official document stating the end of a marriage between a husband and wife. 

*See* Certificate of Divorce.

* **Associated Articles:** Certificate of Divorce (ID: `4870@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Certificate of Divorce (id: 4870)

CERTIFICATE OF DIVORCE
======================

An official document stating the end of a marriage between a husband and wife. The laws given by Moses required this document ([Deuteronomy 24:1–4](https://ref.ly/Deut24:1-Deut24:4); see [Matthew 5:31](https://ref.ly/Matt5:31); [19:7](https://ref.ly/Matt19:7); [Mark 10:4](https://ref.ly/Mark10:4)). This certificate protected a woman's rights in two ways. First, it proved she was no longer married. Second, it prevented her former husband from claiming her marriage payment (the valuable items she brought into the marriage).

One example of the words used in such a document appears in [Hosea 2:2](https://ref.ly/Hos2:2): “She is not My wife, and I am not her husband.” The prophets of the Old Testament used this idea of divorce to show how God felt about his people when they turned away from him ([Isaiah 50:1](https://ref.ly/Isa50:1); [Jeremiah 3:8](https://ref.ly/Jer3:8)). Just as a husband might separate from an unfaithful wife, God showed he was separating himself from his disobedient people.

*See also* Civil Law and Justice; Divorce; Marriage, Marriage Customs.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 50:1; Jeremiah 3:8; Hosea 2:2; Matthew 5:31; Matthew 19:7; Mark 10:4; Deuteronomy 24:1-4
* **Associated Articles:** Civil Law and Justice (ID: `4579@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Divorce (ID: `4871@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Marriage, Marriage Customs (ID: `6955@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Chabris (id: 4463)

CHABRIS
=======

Chabris was the son of Gothoniel. He was one of three civil officers (magistrates) of Bethulia ([Judith 6:14–15](https://ref.ly/Jdt6:14-Jdt6:15); [10:6](https://ref.ly/Jdt10:6)). When the Assyrians attacked the town, Chabris and the other leaders said they would only wait a few more days for God to save them. If nothing happened, they planned to surrender.

Judith rebuked them for this. She told them not to test God or give him a time limit ([Judith 8:9–27](https://ref.ly/Jdt8:9-Jdt8:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Judith 10:6; Judith 6:14-15; Judith 8:9-27


## Chaereas (id: 4464)

CHAEREAS
========

Chaereas was the brother of Timothy. He was the governor (ruler) of the city of Gazara ([2 Maccabees 10:32](https://ref.ly/2Macc10:32)). The Maccabees attacked Gazara and defeated its army. Many people died in the battle, including Chaereas and Timothy (verses [36–37](https://ref.ly/2Macc10:36-2Macc10:37)).

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Maccabees 10:32; 2 Maccabees 10:36-37


## Chaff (id: 4465)

CHAFF
=====

Loose hulls separated from the edible grains by threshing and winnowing. In Bible times, winnowed grain was a common sight. The wind blew away the lighter husks, leaving only the grain. This gave rise to a strong metaphor. It symbolized that good people or nations would survive judgment, but the wicked would not. So, for example, sinners “are like chaff driven off by the wind” ([Psalm 1:4](https://ref.ly/Ps1:4)).

The prophet Isaiah said of the Assyrians, “You conceive chaff; you give birth to stubble. Your breath is a fire that will consume you” ([Isaiah 33:11](https://ref.ly/Isa33:11)). In Nebuchadnezzar’s dream, the world's nations collapse and are like the chaff of the summer threshing floors before the victory of the coming kingdom of God ([Daniel 2:35](https://ref.ly/Dan2:35)).

In the New Testament, it is said that the coming Messiah will “gather His wheat into the barn; but He will burn up the chaff with unquenchable fire” ([Matthew 3:12](https://ref.ly/Matt3:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Psalms 1:4; Isaiah 33:11; Daniel 2:35; Matthew 3:12


## Chalcedony (id: 4466)

CHALCEDONY
==========

Chalcedony is a form of quartz. It can have many colors, but it is often gray or milky white. Agate is a type of chalcedony. Agate often has visible bands, layers, or cloudy patterns in different colors.

Chalcedony was the third foundation stone of the New Jerusalem in John’s vision ([Revelation 21:19](https://ref.ly/Rev21:19)).

*See also* Minerals and Metals; Precious Stones.

* **Associated Passages:** Revelation 21:19
* **Associated Articles:** Minerals and Metals (ID: `7145@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Precious Stones (ID: `8628@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chalcedon (ID: `place:Chalcedon`)

CHALCOL
=======

## Chaldea, Chaldeans (id: 4468)

CHALDEA, CHALDEANS
==================

Chaldea was an ancient region in southeastern Mesopotamia. The Chaldeans were the people who lived there. 

The name “Chaldea” comes from the Chaldean, or Kaldu, tribes. They lived in Babylonia in southeastern Mesopotamia with several other peoples, especially the Sumerians and Akkadians.

After the old Babylonian Empire became part of the Assyrian Empire, the Chaldeans gained control under Nebuchadnezzar. They built the Neo\-Babylonian Empire, which ruled much of the Near East for nearly a century.

Chaldea is also connected with the biblical patriarch Abraham, an important ancestor of the people of Israel. His home in Mesopotamia was “Ur of the Chaldeans” ([Genesis 11:28](https://ref.ly/Gen11:28)).

The Land and People
-------------------

Until the end of the eighth century BC, Chaldea was a small area in southern Babylonia. Within 100 years, the Chaldeans gained power, and Chaldea came to include all of Babylonia.

At that time, Chaldea extended from Baghdad on the Tigris River south to the Persian Gulf. It also extended along the Euphrates River as far as the city of Hit. Chaldea was mainly between the Tigris and Euphrates Rivers. However, it also included the flat land between the Tigris River and the Zagros Mountains to the east. Some of its territory was west of the Euphrates River. The Arabian Desert formed its western border.

Chaldea was rarely more than 64\.4 kilometers (40 miles) wide. It covered an area of about 12,872 square kilometers (8,000 square miles). On a modern map, most of Chaldea would be within Iraq, with its southwestern edge reaching Kuwait.

History
-------

The first known mention of the Chaldeans appears in the Assyrian records of Ashurnasirpal II, who ruled from 885 to 860 BC. Because of this, some scholars suggest that the Chaldeans entered Babylonia around 1000 BC. 

The Chaldeans are often connected with the Semitic Aramean tribes, though they were not the same people. The Arameans were groups who moved from the western deserts into Mesopotamia. The Chaldeans settled mainly in the southern part of Babylonia, near the northern end of the Persian Gulf. They may have lived there for centuries before Assyrian records first mentioned them.

[Job 1:17](https://ref.ly/Job1:17) mentions three groups of Chaldeans who attacked Job’s servants and stole his camels. This probably happened near Edom or northern Arabia. However, this does not necessarily mean that the Chaldeans lived in that area. Centuries earlier, armies from Babylonia, also called Shinar, and from Elam traveled as far as Palestine ([Genesis 14:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen14:1-Gen14:2)).

### Under Assyrian Rule

The Chaldeans lived among the marshes and lakes in the far south. This location helped them remain largely independent, even when Assyria ruled the region. Invading armies found it difficult to move through the Chaldean marshes.

Because of this, the Chaldeans often resisted paying taxes or serving the Assyrian government. When the Assyrians tried to limit their freedom, the Chaldeans used small, irregular attacks and political schemes. They also ignored treaties or changed allies when it helped them.

Many people in the Babylonian cities accepted Assyrian rule. The Chaldeans, however, became leaders of a movement for national independence. For about 250 years, the Assyrians repeatedly had to defend their rule against Chaldean efforts to remain independent and increase their influence.

In 721 BC, the Chaldean leader Marduk\-apla\-iddina II entered Babylon and declared himself king of Babylonia. He is called Merodach\-baladan in [2 Kings 20:12](https://ref.ly/2Kgs20:12) and [Isaiah 39:1](https://ref.ly/Isa39:1). He also sent messengers to Hezekiah, king of Judah. The Assyrian king had long controlled who became king of Babylonia. However, Marduk\-apla\-iddina II kept his claim to the throne for ten years. Then Sargon II of Assyria forced him back into his territory in the south.

After Sargon II died in 705 BC, Marduk\-apla\-iddina II declared himself king again. The new Assyrian king, Sennacherib, defeated him. Sennacherib then destroyed Babylon as a warning to the Chaldeans and their allies.

Sennacherib’s son Esarhaddon became the next king of Assyria. He tried to make peace with the Babylonians and rebuilt Babylon, their capital city. This helped reduce Chaldean resistance and began a period of peace that lasted more than 30 years.

The last unsuccessful revolt took place during the reign of Ashurbanipal. His brother started the revolt. Ashurbanipal had appointed him as king of Babylon. The Chaldeans joined the rebellion, but Assyria defeated it in 648 BC.

### The Neo\-Babylonian Empire

About 20 years later, Ashurbanipal died, and Assyrian power quickly became weaker. Nabopolassar, a Chaldean governor, used this opportunity to drive the Assyrians out of Babylonia. He became king of Babylon in 625 BC.

The Babylonians then joined with the Medes to defeat the Assyrian Empire. They captured the Assyrian capital cities of Asshur in 614 BC and Nineveh in 612 BC. The Babylonians and Medes divided the conquered lands between them. The Babylonians took control of the Assyrian territories west and south of the Tigris River and formed a new Babylonian Empire.

The first Babylonian Empire, which is associated with Hammurabi, had existed more than 1,000 years earlier. During this later period, the names Chaldea and Babylonia came to be used for the same region throughout the Near East.

During the long reign of Nabopolassar’s son Nebuchadnezzar II, also called Nebuchadrezzar II, the Babylonian Empire reached the height of its power. Before he became king, Nebuchadnezzar defeated the Egyptians at Carchemish in 605 BC ([2 Chronicles 35:20](https://ref.ly/2Chr35:20)). This victory helped establish Babylon as the leading power in the Near East. In that same year, the southern kingdom of Judah came under Babylonian rule.

Nebuchadnezzar forced King Jehoiakim to submit to Babylon. He also took valuable objects from the temple in Jerusalem and carried them to his temple in Babylon. In addition, he took some of Judah’s leading men and young people as captives ([2 Kings 24:1](https://ref.ly/2Kgs24:1); [2 Chronicles 36:5–7](https://ref.ly/2Chr36:5-2Chr36:7); [Daniel 1:1–4](https://ref.ly/Dan1:1-Dan1:4)). 

Several years later, Judah rebelled with support from Egypt. The Babylonian army captured Jerusalem in 597 BC. Judah’s new king, Jehoiachin, was removed from the throne, and more of Judah’s leaders were taken away ([2 Kings 24:8–16](https://ref.ly/2Kgs24:8-2Kgs24:16)). A second revolt took place in 594 BC under Zedekiah, the king appointed by Babylon. This led to another Babylonian invasion. Jerusalem was destroyed in 586 BC, and most of the people of Judah were taken into exile ([2 Kings 24:20–25:12](https://ref.ly/2Kgs24:20-2Kgs25:12); [2 Chronicles 36:11–21](https://ref.ly/2Chr36:11-2Chr36:21)). 

Nebuchadnezzar used wealth taken from his conquests to make Babylon one of the most impressive cities of the ancient world. His building projects included the Hanging Gardens, traditionally counted among the seven wonders of the ancient world, the Ishtar Gate, and a defensive outer wall about 27 kilometers (17 miles) long. The book of Daniel says that Nebuchadnezzar became proud of what he had built. Because of this pride, he came under God’s judgment ([Daniel 4:30–33](https://ref.ly/Dan4:30-Dan4:33)).

Nebuchadnezzar was followed as king by his son Amel\-marduk. He is called Evil\-merodach in [2 Kings 25:27](https://ref.ly/2Kgs25:27) and [Jeremiah 52:31](https://ref.ly/Jer52:31). These passages remember him for showing special kindness to Jehoiachin, the exiled king of Judah. After two years, Amel\-marduk was killed in an armed revolt led by his brother\-in\-law, Nergal\-shar\-usur. He is called Nergal\-sharezer in [Jeremiah 39:3](https://ref.ly/Jer39:3). Nergal\-shar\-usur then became king and tried to establish his own family line as rulers.

After ruling for four years, Nergal\-shar\-usur was followed by his son. His son ruled for only a few months before Nabonidus made himself king.

### The Fall of Babylon

Nabonidus was the last Chaldean king. Many Babylonian officials supported him when he became king. They saw that the Medes, who had once been their allies, were becoming a rival power. They believed Nabonidus was strong enough to face this threat.

However, Nabonidus tried to change Babylonian religious practices, and these changes were very unpopular. His efforts to improve the economy also failed. Because of these problems, Nabonidus spent long periods away from Babylon. During one long absence, he appointed his son Belshazzar to rule with him.

This helps explain why the Old Testament book of Daniel calls Belshazzar the king of Babylon. It also explains why Belshazzar could offer to make Daniel only “the third ruler in the kingdom” ([Daniel 5:7](https://ref.ly/Dan5:7)).

While Belshazzar was governing Babylon, the famous event known as the “writing on the wall” took place. The message warned that Babylon would soon fall ([Daniel 5](https://ref.ly/Dan5:1-Dan5:31)). At that time, the Elamites were already attacking the eastern part of the empire. Reports of growing Persian power in the north brought Nabonidus back to Babylon. Soon afterward, Cyrus the Great, king of Persia, invaded.

Cyrus took Babylon without a battle. This ended both Chaldean rule and the Neo\-Babylonian Empire.

Chaldeans as Astrologers
------------------------

Long after the Chaldean Empire ended, people in Hellenistic Egypt, Greece, and Rome continued to use the name “Chaldeans.” They used it for magicians, astrologers, and diviners (people who tried to predict the future). The book of Daniel uses the term in a similar way. It connects the Chaldeans with magicians, enchanters, and astrologers ([Daniel 2:2](https://ref.ly/Dan2:2), [10](https://ref.ly/Dan2:10); [4:7](https://ref.ly/Dan4:7); [5:7](https://ref.ly/Dan5:7)).

The Babylonians had long been known for their advanced knowledge of astronomy, the study of stars and other objects in the sky. They also looked to the stars to help them predict the future. One Babylonian text from about 700 BC described the zodiac, a group of constellations along the path of the sun. It named 15 constellations. Several of these names are still used by astrologers today, including the Bull, the Twins (Gemini), the Scorpion, and Capricorn.

In [Daniel 2:2](https://ref.ly/Dan2:2) and [4:7](https://ref.ly/Dan4:7), one term connected with the Chaldeans comes from a Babylonian word for a group of priests who used incantations (spoken words believed to have special power). Excavated tablets show that these priests received special training. This shows that they had an important role in society. The most outstanding young men of Judah, including Daniel, were chosen for a similar program of education ([Daniel 1:4](https://ref.ly/Dan1:4)).

*See also* Assyria, Assyrians; Astrology; Babylon, Babylonia; Book of Daniel; Dispersion of the Jews, Diaspora; Nebuchadnezzar, Nebuchadrezzar; Ur (Place).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 11:28; 2 Kings 20:12; 2 Kings 24:1; 2 Kings 24:7; 2 Kings 25:27; 2 Chronicles 35:20; Job 1:17; Isaiah 39:1; Jeremiah 39:3; Jeremiah 52:31; Daniel 5:7; Genesis 14:1-2; 2 Chronicles 36:5-7; Daniel 1:1-4; 2 Kings 24:8-16; 2 Kings 24:20-25:12; 2 Chronicles 36:11-21; Daniel 4:30-33; Daniel 5:1-31
* **Associated Articles:** Assyria, Assyrians (ID: `3841@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Astrology (ID: `3845@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Nebuchadnezzar, Nebuchadrezzar (ID: `7332@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Book of Daniel (ID: `4746@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Dispersion of the Jews, Diaspora (ID: `4831@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Babylon, Babylonia (ID: `3957@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Ur (Place) (ID: `8980@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chaldea (ID: `place:Chaldea`); Chaldeans (ID: `group:Chaldea`)

## Chalkstone (id: 4469)

CHALKSTONE
==========

Chalkstone literally means “stones of chalk.” The King James Version uses the term to describe the complete destruction of pagan altars in Judah ([Isaiah 27:9](https://ref.ly/Isa27:9)). Chalk covers many of the hills of Judah and breaks down easily. This makes it a fitting picture of altars being crushed and destroyed.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 27:9


## Chalphi (id: 4470)

CHALPHI
=======

Chalphi was the father of Judas. This Judas was one of two captains in Jonathan Maccabeus’s army. 

During the battle of Gennesaret, the army was ambushed. Many soldiers ran away, but Judas and the other captain, Mattathias, stayed and fought bravely ([1 Maccabees 11:67–74](https://ref.ly/1Macc11:67-1Macc11:74)). Jonathan, Judas, and Mattathias encouraged the troops. Together, they drove back the enemy.

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Maccabees 11:67-74


## Chamberlain (id: 4471)

CHAMBERLAIN
===========

A royal official responsible for the king's private quarters. They sometimes had important tasks and had influence with those in power ([Acts 12:20](https://ref.ly/Acts12:20)). The chamberlain Erastus was actually the city treasurer ([Romans 16:23](https://ref.ly/Rom16:23)). Nathan\-melech the chamberlain was a court official at the time of Josiah ([2 Kings 23:11](https://ref.ly/2Kgs23:11)). The Persian kings used eunuchs (men who were physically unable to have children) as chamberlains ([Esther 1:10, 12, 15](https://ref.ly/Esth1:10,Esth1:12,Esth1:15); [2:3, 14–15](https://ref.ly/Esth2:3,Esth2:14-Esth2:15); [4:4–5](https://ref.ly/Esth4:4-Esth4:5); [6:2, 14](https://ref.ly/Esth6:2,Esth6:14); [7:9](https://ref.ly/Esth7:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Kings 23:11; Esther 1:10; Esther 1:12; Esther 1:15; Esther 2:3; Esther 6:2; Esther 6:14; Esther 7:9; Acts 12:20; Romans 16:23; Esther 2:14-15; Esther 4:4-5


## Chambers of the South (id: 4472)

CHAMBERS OF THE SOUTH
=====================

“Chambers of the South” is a phrase used in the King James Version of [Job 9:9](https://ref.ly/Job9:9). It may refer to a group of stars in the southern sky or to the wide areas of the southern sky where few stars can be seen.

*See* Astronomy.

* **Associated Passages:** Job 9:9
* **Associated Articles:** Astronomy (ID: `3846@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Chameleon (id: 4473)

CHAMELEON
=========

A kind of lizard known for being able to change color quickly. Chameleons can move their eyes separately, so one eye can look up while the other looks down. They live in trees and bushes and use their long tails to hold onto branches.

In the Old Testament, chameleons are listed as ceremonially unclean animals. This means the Israelites were not allowed to eat them ([Leviticus 11:30](https://ref.ly/Lev11:30)). The Hebrew word translated as “chameleon” may come from a word that means "to pant." This might be because of the way the animal breathes. Some ancient people believed lizards lived by breathing air alone.

*See also* Lizard.

* **Associated Passages:** Leviticus 11:30
* **Associated Articles:** Animals (ID: `3614@TyndaleBibleDictionary`); Lizard (ID: `6763@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chameleon (ID: `fauna:Chameleon`)

## Chamois (id: 4474)

CHAMOIS
=======

Chamois is the name of a small, goatlike antelope that lives mainly in the high mountains of Europe. However, in [Deuteronomy 14:5](https://ref.ly/Deut14:5), the King James Version uses “chamois” as an inaccurate translation of a Hebrew word that is better translated “mountain sheep.”

*See* Sheep.

* **Associated Passages:** Deuteronomy 14:5
* **Associated Articles:** Sheep (ID: `8328@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Chanaan (id: 4475)

CHANAAN
=======

The King James Version spelling of Canaan in [Acts 7:11](https://ref.ly/Acts7:11) and [13:19](https://ref.ly/Acts13:19).

*See* Canaan, Canaanite.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 7:11; Acts 13:19

## Channels of the Sea (id: 4476)

CHANNELS OF THE SEA
===================

Valleys or stream beds in the ocean floor. The Lord saved David from all his enemies and King Saul. Then David praised the great power of God. This power could expose the ocean floor with a blast of his breath ([2 Samuel 22:16](https://ref.ly/2Sam22:16); [Psalm 18:15](https://ref.ly/Ps18:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Samuel 22:16; Psalms 18:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Foundation (ID: `realia:Foundation`)

## Chant (id: 4477)

CHANT
=====

A chant is a type of singing or speaking in a repeated, rhythmic way that often sounds like one continuous tone ([Psalm 8:1](https://ref.ly/Ps8:1); [Ezekiel 32:16](https://ref.ly/Ezek32:16)). 

*See* Music.

* **Associated Passages:** Psalms 8:1; Ezekiel 32:16
* **Associated Articles:** Music (ID: `7248@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Chaphenatha (id: 4479)

CHAPHENATHA
===========

Chaphenatha was a section of Jerusalem that Jonathan Maccabeus repaired to protect the city from enemy attack ([1 Maccabees 12:37](https://ref.ly/1Macc12:37)). 

Its exact location is uncertain. Some scholars think the name should be read as “Chapheltha,” another name for the Mishneh, or Second Quarter of Jerusalem (compare [2 Kings 22:14](https://ref.ly/2Kgs22:14), [Zephaniah 1:10](https://ref.ly/Zeph1:10)). Others think Chaphenatha means "the bend of the fountain" and refers to an area near the pool of Siloam. There is not enough evidence to decide between these views.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Kings 22:14; Zephaniah 1:10; 1 Maccabees 12:37


## Chapiter (id: 4480)

CHAPITER
========

The word “chapiter” is used in the King James Version to mean “capital.” A capital is the top part of a pillar or column in a building.

*See* Capital.

* **Associated Articles:** Capital (ID: `4420@TyndaleBibleDictionary`)


## Charashim (id: 4481)

CHARASHIM
=========

The King James Version form of Ge\-harashim, a valley on the plain of Sharon’s southern border, in [1 Chronicles 4:14](https://ref.ly/1Chr4:14). 

*See* Ge\-harashim.

* **Associated Passages:** 1 Chronicles 4:14

## Charax (id: 4482)

CHARAX
======

Charax was a place that may have been in the land of Tob. Judas Maccabeus and his army passed through Charax while chasing their Syrian enemies ([2 Maccabees 12:17–19](https://ref.ly/2Macc12:17-2Macc12:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Maccabees 12:17-19


## Charchemish (id: 4483)

CHARCHEMISH
===========
